Cross</a></li><li><i><a href="/wiki/Croix_de_guerre_1914%E2%80%931918_(France)" title="Croix de guerre 1914–1918 (France)">Croix de guerre</a></i> (France)</li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Stanley Melbourne Bruce, 1st Viscount Bruce of Melbourne</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Companion_of_the_Order_of_the_Companions_of_Honour" class="mw-redirect" title="Companion of the Order of the Companions of Honour">CH</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Military_Cross" title="Military Cross">MC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Her_Majesty%27s_Most_Honourable_Privy_Council" class="mw-redirect" title="Her Majesty&#39;s Most Honourable Privy Council">PC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society" title="Fellow of the Royal Society">FRS</a></span> (15 April 1883&#160;– 25 August 1967) was an Australian politician, statesman and businessman who served as the eighth <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Australia">prime minister of Australia</a>, from 1923 to 1929, holding office as the leader of the <a href="/wiki/Nationalist_Party_(Australia)" title="Nationalist Party (Australia)">Nationalist Party</a>. He previously served as the <a href="/wiki/Treasurer_of_Australia" title="Treasurer of Australia">treasurer of Australia</a> from 1921 to 1923.
</p><p>Born into a briefly wealthy <a href="/wiki/Melbourne" title="Melbourne">Melbourne</a> family, Bruce studied at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Cambridge" title="University of Cambridge">University of Cambridge</a> and played a leading role in his family's softgoods firm following the suicide of his father <a href="/wiki/John_Munro_Bruce" title="John Munro Bruce">John Munro Bruce</a>. He served on the front lines of the <a href="/wiki/Gallipoli_Campaign" class="mw-redirect" title="Gallipoli Campaign">Gallipoli Campaign</a> in World War I and returned to Australia wounded in 1917, becoming a spokesman for government recruitment efforts. He gained the attention of the Nationalist Party and prime minister <a href="/wiki/Billy_Hughes" title="Billy Hughes">Billy Hughes</a>, who encouraged a political career. He was elected to the <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Australian House of Representatives">House of Representatives</a> in 1918, becoming member of parliament (MP) for the seat of <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Flinders" title="Division of Flinders">Flinders</a>. He was appointed as <a href="/wiki/Treasurer_of_Australia" title="Treasurer of Australia">treasurer</a> in 1921, before replacing Hughes as prime minister in 1923. He established an anti-socialist coalition government with the agrarian <a href="/wiki/Australian_Country_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian Country Party">Country Party</a>, working closely with Country leader <a href="/wiki/Earle_Page" title="Earle Page">Earle Page</a> in an arrangement that pioneered the modern <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">Liberal–National coalition</a>.
</p><p>In office, Bruce pursued an energetic and diverse agenda. He comprehensively overhauled federal government administration and oversaw its transfer to the new capital city of <a href="/wiki/Canberra" title="Canberra">Canberra</a>. He implemented various reforms to the <a href="/wiki/Federalism_in_Australia" title="Federalism in Australia">Australian federal system</a> to strengthen the role of the Commonwealth, and helped develop the forerunners of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Federal_Police" title="Australian Federal Police">Australian Federal Police</a> and the <a href="/wiki/CSIRO" title="CSIRO">CSIRO</a>. Bruce's "men, money and markets" scheme was an ambitious attempt to rapidly expand Australia's population and economic potential through massive government investment and closer ties with Great Britain and the rest of the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a>. However, his endeavours to overhaul Australia's industrial relations system brought his government into frequent conflict with the labour movement, and his radical proposal to abolish the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Court_of_Conciliation_and_Arbitration" title="Commonwealth Court of Conciliation and Arbitration">Commonwealth Court of Conciliation and Arbitration</a> in 1929 prompted members of his own party into <a href="/wiki/Crossing_the_floor" title="Crossing the floor">crossing the floor</a> to defeat the government. In the resounding loss at the <a href="/wiki/1929_Australian_federal_election" title="1929 Australian federal election">1929 election</a>, Bruce lost his own seat, making him the only sitting prime minister to lose his seat until <a href="/wiki/John_Howard" title="John Howard">John Howard</a>'s defeat at the <a href="/wiki/2007_Australian_federal_election" title="2007 Australian federal election">2007 election</a>.
</p><p>Although he returned to parliament in 1931, Bruce's service in the <a href="/wiki/Lyons_government" title="Lyons government">Lyons government</a> was brief. Instead he pursued an international career, accepting appointment as High Commissioner to the United Kingdom in 1933. Bruce became an influential figure in British government circles and at the <a href="/wiki/League_of_Nations" title="League of Nations">League of Nations</a>, emerging as a tireless advocate for international co-operation on economic and social problems, especially those facing the <a href="/wiki/Developing_world" class="mw-redirect" title="Developing world">developing world</a>. Particularly passionate on improving global nutrition, Bruce was one of the key figures in the establishment of the Food and Agriculture Organization, serving as the first chairman of its governing council. He was the first Australian to sit in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a>, as well as the first Chancellor of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_National_University" title="Australian National University">Australian National University</a>. Although his diplomatic career went largely unnoticed in Australia, he continued throughout his life in London to vociferously advocate for Australian interests (particularly during World War II) and asked that his remains be scattered over Canberra when he died.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Paterson,_Laing_and_Bruce.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Paterson%2C_Laing_and_Bruce.jpg/220px-Paterson%2C_Laing_and_Bruce.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Paterson%2C_Laing_and_Bruce.jpg/330px-Paterson%2C_Laing_and_Bruce.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Paterson%2C_Laing_and_Bruce.jpg/440px-Paterson%2C_Laing_and_Bruce.jpg 2x" data-file-width="557" data-file-height="373" /></a><figcaption>The firm of Paterson, Laing and Bruce, 1900. John Bruce sits at the centre.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Stanley Melbourne Bruce was born on 15 April 1883 in <a href="/wiki/St_Kilda,_Victoria" title="St Kilda, Victoria">St Kilda, Victoria</a>, a suburb of Melbourne, and was the youngest of five children.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECarroll105_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarroll105-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> He disliked his given name and throughout his life preferred to be known by his initials "S.M.", even among close friends. His wife called him simply "S".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston1_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston1-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> When he became prime minister he issued a note to the press asking newspapers to use his initials and not his given name.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bruce's father, <a href="/wiki/John_Munro_Bruce" title="John Munro Bruce">John Munro Bruce</a>, was born to Scottish parents in <a href="/wiki/County_Leitrim" title="County Leitrim">County Leitrim</a>, Ireland, and had emigrated to Australia in 1858 at the age of 18. His mother, Mary Ann Henderson, was Irish and had married her cousin John after emigrating to Australia in 1872 at the age of 24.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee1–2_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee1–2-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>  John Bruce became a talented businessman with "a flair for buying and selling",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee2_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> which would secure him a partnership in an established Melbourne importing firm that in 1868 became known as Paterson, Laing and Bruce.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards8_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards8-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> As his wealth grew, John Bruce became influential in colonial Victoria's social and political life. An avid golfer, he was one of the founders of the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Melbourne_Golf_Club" title="Royal Melbourne Golf Club">Royal Melbourne Golf Club</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards9–10_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards9–10-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> He was prominent in the <a href="/wiki/Protectionist_Party" title="Protectionist Party">liberal protectionist</a> political movement within the state and an early supporter of future prime minister <a href="/wiki/Alfred_Deakin" title="Alfred Deakin">Alfred Deakin</a>. John Bruce's success ensured that Stanley Bruce, his sister Mary and his brothers Ernest, William and Robert were born into affluence. Shortly after Stanley Bruce's birth the family relocated to the stately Wombalano manor, built by John Bruce, in <a href="/wiki/Toorak,_Victoria" title="Toorak, Victoria">Toorak</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards8–9_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards8–9-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> However, John Bruce was an aloof and remote figure in the lives of his children, as his son Stanley later recounted.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee3_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee3-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Despite their family's <a href="/wiki/Presbyterian" class="mw-redirect" title="Presbyterian">Presbyterian</a> background, Stanley Bruce was sent to Melbourne Church of England Grammar School (now <a href="/wiki/Melbourne_Grammar_School" title="Melbourne Grammar School">Melbourne Grammar School</a>) and subsequently Stanley Bruce would come to identify principally as <a href="/wiki/Anglican" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglican">Anglican</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee3–4_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee3–4-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Bruce was an average student but extremely active in the sporting life of the school, captain of its <a href="/wiki/Australian_football" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian football">Australian football</a> team, and then of the school itself in 1901. Today, the school honours him with his own house, Bruce House, the colours of which are scarlet and white. The house's mascot is a lion, symbolising Bruce's bravery.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee3–4_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee3–4-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The economic depression of the 1880s and 1890s hit the Bruce family fortunes hard. John Bruce lost much of his fortune in the Victorian bank collapse of 1894 and incurred large debts to buy out his partners in the importing business in 1897.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStirling162_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStirling162-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The family suffered a great deal more tragedy over the coming decades. Stanley's brother William committed suicide in 1899, shortly after seeking treatment for mental illness.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee4_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee4-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Just two years later John Bruce took his own life during a business trip to Paris; he had suffered from depression as a result of the great pressures on his business and finances.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee4–5_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee4–5-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>  His sister Mary endured a long illness before succumbing in 1908, and his mother died too in 1912. Finally, Bruce's beloved brother Ernest the recipient, like Stanley, of an MC for bravery,  shot himself in 1919, suffering from physical and mental injuries sustained during his military service in World War I.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee4–5_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee4–5-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Portrait_of_S.M._Bruce.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/Portrait_of_S.M._Bruce.png/170px-Portrait_of_S.M._Bruce.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/Portrait_of_S.M._Bruce.png/255px-Portrait_of_S.M._Bruce.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/Portrait_of_S.M._Bruce.png/340px-Portrait_of_S.M._Bruce.png 2x" data-file-width="448" data-file-height="579" /></a><figcaption>Bruce in the 1910s</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the aftermath of his father's death in 1901, the family fortunes were at a low ebb and Bruce went into the family business after leaving high school. The young Bruce was ambitious and determined to get an education. With loaned money, he moved to the United Kingdom with his mother and sister and enrolled in <a href="/wiki/Trinity_Hall,_Cambridge" title="Trinity Hall, Cambridge">Trinity Hall, Cambridge</a> in 1902.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee6_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee6-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> He was a popular if average student, heavily involved in the athletic life of the college, including as a member of the Cambridge rowing crew that won <a href="/wiki/The_Boat_Race" title="The Boat Race">the Boat Race</a> in 1904.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Rowing remained one of his great passions, and he continued to coach crews (including several for the <a href="/wiki/Henley_Royal_Regatta" title="Henley Royal Regatta">Henley Royal Regatta</a>) and write on the subject for much of his life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards15–28_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards15–28-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Ernest Bruce had remained in Australia to take charge of the family's business interests. In 1906, he lobbied the directors of the company to have his brother Stanley take over the chairmanship of Paterson, Laing and Bruce, and was ultimately successful. Despite being just 23, he proved an able chairman, and with Stanley in London managing the exporting and financial interests, and Ernest managing the importation and sales operations in Melbourne, the financial fortunes of the business and the family rapidly recovered.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee6–7_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee6–7-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> During these years, Bruce also trained and worked as a solicitor and then as a <a href="/wiki/Barrister" title="Barrister">barrister</a> in the London with the firm of <a href="/wiki/Ashurst_LLP" title="Ashurst LLP">Ashurst, Morris, Crisp &amp; Co</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards16–17_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards16–17-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> His work for the firm took him to Mexico in 1908 and <a href="/wiki/Colombia" title="Colombia">Colombia</a> in 1912, which fostered an interest in international affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By 1912 Bruce was a businessman and a successful barrister, and it was in this year <a href="/wiki/Ethel_Bruce" title="Ethel Bruce">Ethel Dunlop Anderson</a> travelled to England and was reacquainted with Bruce, whom she had known as a child. Aged 32, Ethel was of similar Scottish-Irish ancestry and hailed from a prominent <a href="/wiki/Squatting_(pastoral)" class="mw-redirect" title="Squatting (pastoral)">squatter</a> family of Victoria. She shared many of Bruce's interests, especially golf, and his political outlook. They married in July 1913 in a quiet ceremony.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards28–29_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards28–29-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Theirs was a close-knit relationship&#160;&#8211;&#32; they would have many acquaintances but a small circle of close friends, and their relationship was one of mutual devotion.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee8–9_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee8–9-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> But the death of all but one member of his immediate family in just over a decade, and the fact that the Bruces would bear no children of their own, deeply affected Bruce. His brother Ernest's daughter, Helen Bruce, came to play a large part in his life and was to become the main beneficiary of his Will but Bruce "was left with a sense of insecurity and melancholy".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee14_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee14-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Military_service">Military service</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Military service">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sbruce.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Sbruce.jpg/170px-Sbruce.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="255" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Sbruce.jpg/255px-Sbruce.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Sbruce.jpg/340px-Sbruce.jpg 2x" data-file-width="377" data-file-height="566" /></a><figcaption>Captain Bruce of the Royal Fusiliers during World War I</figcaption></figure>
<p>Bruce returned briefly to Australia in 1914, swapping positions within the company with his brother Ernest. World War I broke out in August of that year. Bruce and his brothers sought to enlist in defence of the Empire, but all three of them would choose to serve in the British Army rather than the <a href="/wiki/First_Australian_Imperial_Force" title="First Australian Imperial Force">Australian Imperial Force</a>. It was easier to obtain officer commissions in the British Army and the family had a close association with (and for many of them, long periods of residency in) Great Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee9_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee9-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Bruce enlisted and received a commission as a <a href="/wiki/Lieutenant_(British_Army)" class="mw-redirect" title="Lieutenant (British Army)">lieutenant</a> on 7 February 1915<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> and was attached to the 2nd Battalion, <a href="/wiki/Royal_Fusiliers" title="Royal Fusiliers">Royal Fusiliers</a> in Egypt, which was then assigned to the <a href="/wiki/British_29th_Division" class="mw-redirect" title="British 29th Division">British 29th Division</a>. The 29th subsequently joined operations in Turkey along with <a href="/wiki/Australian_and_New_Zealand_Army_Corps" title="Australian and New Zealand Army Corps">Australian and New Zealand Army Corps</a> (ANZAC) troops that year under Sir <a href="/wiki/Ian_Standish_Monteith_Hamilton" class="mw-redirect" title="Ian Standish Monteith Hamilton">Ian Hamilton</a>, commander of the <a href="/wiki/Mediterranean_Expeditionary_Force" title="Mediterranean Expeditionary Force">Mediterranean Expeditionary Force</a> (MEF).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee10_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee10-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/First_Lord_of_the_Admiralty" title="First Lord of the Admiralty">First Lord of the Admiralty</a> <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> had devised a strategy of capturing the <a href="/wiki/Dardanelles" title="Dardanelles">Dardanelles</a> from Turkey to allow naval access to Allied Russia. However <a href="/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I" title="Allies of World War I">allied naval forces</a> were unable to secure passage through the narrow straits, and the MEF under Hamilton devised a plan to wrest control through amphibious landings. This was the beginning of the now infamous <a href="/wiki/Gallipoli_Campaign" class="mw-redirect" title="Gallipoli Campaign">Gallipoli Campaign</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bruce's regiment landed at <a href="/wiki/Cape_Helles" title="Cape Helles">Cape Helles</a> in mid-1915, where he fully distinguished himself in the <a href="/wiki/Trench_warfare" title="Trench warfare">construction of trenches</a> and as an able commander.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards31–32_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards31–32-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> His battalion suffered heavy casualties over the coming months, and Bruce himself was wounded on 3 June by a shot to the arm, though it was this injury that spared him from a major assault by his battalion on 4 June in which many of his peers perished. He later reflected that he must have been kept on earth for some purpose.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStirling485_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStirling485-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> He returned to the front lines and his division moved to the new front at <a href="/wiki/Suvla_Bay" class="mw-redirect" title="Suvla Bay">Suvla Bay</a>, where it was involved in particularly heavy fighting and sustained trench warfare throughout August and September. Bruce received the <a href="/wiki/Military_Cross" title="Military Cross">Military Cross</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Croix_de_Guerre" title="Croix de Guerre">Croix de Guerre</a> for his service during these months,<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> and rose to the rank of <a href="/wiki/Captain_(British_Army)" class="mw-redirect" title="Captain (British Army)">captain</a> on 5 August.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee12_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee12-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> He was wounded again on 26 September, this time by a shot to the knee, which left him crippled for several years and forced him to return to England to recuperate, while the rest of his regiment were transferred to France after the abandonment of the Gallipoli campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee13_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee13-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although it had been the agreement before the war that Ernest would stay and manage Paterson, Laing and Bruce while his brothers were serving, Ernest Bruce decided to enlist in the British Army in 1915. Hence, in September 1916 Bruce sought to resign his commission and return to Australia to resume management of the family business.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards33_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards33-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/War_Office" title="War Office">War Office</a> refused his request but granted him leave to return to Australia while recuperating from his injuries. As a decorated soldier on crutches with a gift for public speaking, he was enlisted to become a spokesperson for government recruitment in Australia. His success and popularity in this role brought the attention of the Nationalist League and then Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Billy_Hughes" title="Billy Hughes">Billy Hughes</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECarroll106_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarroll106-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> who lobbied the British government on his behalf and succeeded in convincing the War Office to allow Bruce to relinquish his commission in June 1917.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Having served with many of his countrymen he returned to Australia with a renewed sense of pride and mission in the country of his birth.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee13_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee13-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> But having borne witness to the catastrophic loss of life in the Gallipoli Campaign and the death of most of his army comrades, as well as having suffered through the loss of most of his family, at age 34 Bruce was imbued with "a driving ambition to make something of a life which providence had spared".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee14_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee14-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_years_in_politics">Early years in politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Early years in politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Bruce's popularity as a speaker for government recruitment efforts also earned him the attention of the National Union of Victoria, an influential group of Melbourne businessmen who provided much of the financing for the federal <a href="/wiki/Nationalist_Party_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Nationalist Party of Australia">Nationalist Party</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee17_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee17-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> As Sir <a href="/wiki/William_Irvine_(Australian_politician)" title="William Irvine (Australian politician)">William Irvine</a> had recently resigned from parliament to become <a href="/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_Victoria" title="Chief Justice of Victoria">Chief Justice of Victoria</a>, there was to be <a href="/wiki/1918_Flinders_by-election" title="1918 Flinders by-election">a by-election</a> for the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Flinders" title="Division of Flinders">Division of Flinders</a> in 1918, which the National Union asked Bruce to stand for.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee17_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee17-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> The Nationalist <a href="/wiki/Preselection" title="Preselection">preselection</a> for the seat attracted many candidates, and <a href="/wiki/Edward_Fancourt_Mitchell" title="Edward Fancourt Mitchell">Sir Edward Mitchell</a>, one of Australia's leading constitutional lawyers, was presumed to be the front runner. Mitchell proved to be a disappointing speaker, and Bruce won preselection with the help of the National Union. A deal arranged by acting Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/William_Watt_(Australian_politician)" title="William Watt (Australian politician)">William Watt</a> prevented the newly formed <a href="/wiki/National_Party_of_Australia" title="National Party of Australia">Country Party</a> from challenging Bruce in what was a partly rural seat, stretching from <a href="/wiki/Dandenong" class="mw-redirect" title="Dandenong">Dandenong</a> out into the rural areas south and east of Melbourne. In the by-election of 11 May, Bruce easily defeated his Labor opponent Gordon Holmes.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Speaking to the delegates in Dandenong, Bruce summed up his political philosophy:
</p>
<blockquote><p>A plain soldier and business man. I am no politician, nor have I any desire to be one. In the course of my commercial career it has been my fate to have had much experience of politicians and their ways. What I have seen in the course of that experience has given me little respect either for the professional politician or his methods. I am desirous of seeing this country governed in the ways of clear common sense and good sound business principles, and I think that desire of mine is heartily shared by the vast majority of the population.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee18_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee18-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Stanley_Bruce_and_Billy_Hughes,_1925.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Stanley_Bruce_and_Billy_Hughes%2C_1925.png/220px-Stanley_Bruce_and_Billy_Hughes%2C_1925.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Stanley_Bruce_and_Billy_Hughes%2C_1925.png/330px-Stanley_Bruce_and_Billy_Hughes%2C_1925.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Stanley_Bruce_and_Billy_Hughes%2C_1925.png/440px-Stanley_Bruce_and_Billy_Hughes%2C_1925.png 2x" data-file-width="832" data-file-height="557" /></a><figcaption>Billy Hughes, <a href="/wiki/Herbert_Pratten" title="Herbert Pratten">Herbert Pratten</a> and Stanley Bruce</figcaption></figure>
<p>Bruce's early years in parliament were unremarkable and his energies were primarily focused on the affairs of Paterson, Laing and Bruce. However, in 1921 he attracted the attention of his parliamentary colleagues over the matter of the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Line" title="Commonwealth Line">Commonwealth Line</a>. Nationalist Prime Minister Billy Hughes, now a peace-time leader, had declined in popularity within the party due to his left-wing domestic policies.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESawer156_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESawer156-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The Commonwealth Line had been created by the Hughes government as a state-owned concern to ship Australian goods during World War I when British and domestic commercial shipping were unavailable. However its post-war existence was extensively criticised by Bruce as inappropriate and inefficient, and many of his economically conservative colleagues agreed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFitzhardinge500–501_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFitzhardinge500–501-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Bruce also distinguished himself as one of Australia's two representatives to the <a href="/wiki/League_of_Nations" title="League of Nations">League of Nations</a> in 1921 in <a href="/wiki/Geneva" title="Geneva">Geneva</a>, at which he passionately advocated for <a href="/wiki/Disarmament" title="Disarmament">disarmament</a> and greater international co-operation, despite his general scepticism with regards to the League's mission and potential for success.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee20–21_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee20–21-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Treasurer.2C_1921.E2.80.931923"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Treasurer,_1921–1923">Treasurer, 1921–1923</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Treasurer, 1921–1923">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Returning from Europe in October 1921, he was invited by Prime Minister Billy Hughes to join his government as <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Trade_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister for Trade (Australia)">Minister for Trade and Customs</a>. Bruce was not interested&#160;&#8211;&#32; he was the head of one of Australia's largest importing houses and thus had a serious <a href="/wiki/Conflict_of_interest" title="Conflict of interest">conflict of interest</a> with the portfolio, as well as being very busy running the affairs of that business. But he countered by stating that he might feel obliged to accept if the position of <a href="/wiki/Treasurer_of_Australia" title="Treasurer of Australia">Treasurer</a> was offered, knowing that it had already been promised to <a href="/wiki/Walter_Massy-Greene" title="Walter Massy-Greene">Walter Massy-Greene</a>. To Bruce's surprise, Hughes agreed, although Massy-Greene was to remain the number two in the government and <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Defence_(Australia)" title="Minister for Defence (Australia)">Minister for Defence</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards57–58_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards57–58-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Bruce had only been in parliament for three years, yet his business background was highly desirable to Hughes, who was facing growing criticism from the pro-business figures of the party. These figures had become increasingly suspicious of Hughes and his interventionist approach to the economy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards59–60_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards59–60-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Influential party figures such as Senator <a href="/wiki/George_Pearce" title="George Pearce">George Pearce</a> and the National Union had also identified Bruce as their preferred choice for the treasury.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bruce and Hughes clashed in both style and ideology. Bruce found Hughes' management of the government capricious and chaotic, and felt that little was accomplished in Cabinet or in party meetings so long as Hughes headed them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards63–65_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards63–65-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> But he was a strong counterweight to the domineering Hughes, resisting several of his more expensive proposals or acting as the voice of reason to talk Hughes down from several of his more outlandish ideas.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETreasury_of_Australia_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETreasury_of_Australia-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> His tenure would ultimately be short, presiding over just one budget in 1922, which was conservative and tax-cutting. The <a href="/wiki/Opposition_(Australia)" title="Opposition (Australia)">Opposition</a> criticised the budget for its failure to limit rising government spending and indebtedness.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> The budget included many concessions to rural interests and the recently formed Country Party, which appeared as a major threat to Nationalist Party dominance going into the <a href="/wiki/1922_Australian_federal_election" title="1922 Australian federal election">1922 elections</a> in December.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards67_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards67-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Yet Bruce endeared himself to many of his colleagues with his amiable personal style, his forceful voice in Cabinet against Hughes, and his conservative views, which were more in line with the majority of the party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards69–71_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards69–71-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister.2C_1923.E2.80.931929"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister,_1923–1929">Prime Minister, 1923–1929</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Prime Minister, 1923–1929">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Nla1ndBruceMinistry.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Nla1ndBruceMinistry.jpg/310px-Nla1ndBruceMinistry.jpg" decoding="async" width="310" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/c8/Nla1ndBruceMinistry.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="408" data-file-height="299" /></a><figcaption>Bruce's newly assembled ministry, 1923</figcaption></figure>
<p>The Nationalists lost eleven seats and their majority in the <a href="/wiki/Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Australian House of Representatives">Australian House of Representatives</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1922_Australian_federal_election" title="1922 Australian federal election">1922 election</a>. Only three of these seats were picked up by the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Labor Party</a> though, and Labor still lacked the numbers to form government in their own right. Rather, a breakaway anti-Hughes <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(1922)" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Party (1922)">Liberal Party</a> took five government seats, while the Country Party increased their number to 14 and now held the balance of power.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber27_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber27-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> The only politically realistic option for the Nationalists to stay in office was to come to an agreement with the Country Party.  However, Country Party leader <a href="/wiki/Earle_Page" title="Earle Page">Earle Page</a> refused to support a Nationalist government with Hughes as prime minister, and negotiations throughout January and February failed to break the impasse.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFitzhardinge511–517_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFitzhardinge511–517-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>  Rather than risk being defeated in the legislature, which might have resulted in the <a href="/wiki/Governor-General_of_Australia" title="Governor-General of Australia">Governor-General</a> asking Labor to form government, Hughes surprised his colleagues by announcing his intention to resign on 2 February.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFitzhardinge515–517_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFitzhardinge515–517-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> With deputy leader <a href="/wiki/Walter_Massy-Greene" title="Walter Massy-Greene">Walter Massy-Greene</a> having lost his seat at the 1922 election, Hughes now sent for Bruce to take over as leader of the party. After some reluctance, Bruce finally agreed, although Hughes later regretted the decision and became one of the new prime minister's most outspoken detractors.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee24–25_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee24–25-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards75_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards75-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bruce moved quickly to secure a working majority for his government. He convinced Hughes' long time political ally George Pearce to join his ministry and shored up the support of the other former <a href="/wiki/National_Labor_Party" title="National Labor Party">National Labor</a> members of the Nationalist Party whom had walked out of the Labor Party with Hughes in 1916. He appointed William Watt as <a href="/wiki/Speaker_of_the_Australian_House_of_Representatives" title="Speaker of the Australian House of Representatives">Speaker of the House</a>, effectively removing one of his key opponents from the benches, a tactical manoeuvre that became common in Australian politics thereafter.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReid_&amp;_Forrest37–38_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReid_&amp;_Forrest37–38-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> But his most lasting political achievement was the negotiation of what became known as the <a href="/wiki/Coalition_(Australia)" title="Coalition (Australia)">Coalition</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32; an arrangement of electoral and political co-operation between the Nationalists (and their successors) and the Country Party. Although differing greatly in character and background, the orderly and diplomatic Bruce forged a strong working relationship with the intelligent but irascible Country Party leader Page.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston30–31_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston30–31-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Bruce had to pay a very high price for this relationship, however.  As part of the Coalition agreement, the Country Party received five seats in a Cabinet of 11.  Page also became Treasurer and ranked second in the Cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> The Nationalists also made major concessions on rural development and taxation policy&#160;&#8211;&#32; compromises that stirred some resentment among some members of Bruce's party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESouter194_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESouter194-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESouter202–203_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESouter202–203-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>  Page, who would serve as Treasurer and <i>de facto</i> <a href="/wiki/Deputy_Prime_Minister_of_Australia" title="Deputy Prime Minister of Australia">Deputy Prime Minister</a> throughout Bruce's tenure, would become a great admirer of Bruce, stating, "He was a leader who impressed his colleagues with his sincerity and his capacity, and earned their loyalty as the reward for his wisdom and integrity."<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bruce took office on 9 February.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography_20-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> His appointment as prime minister marked an important turning point in Australian political history. He was the first prime minister who had not been involved in the movement for <a href="/wiki/Federation_of_Australia" title="Federation of Australia">Federation</a>, who had not been a member of a colonial or state parliament, and who had not been a member of the original <a href="/wiki/1901_Australian_federal_election" title="1901 Australian federal election">1901 federal parliament</a>. He was, in addition, the first prime minister to head a cabinet consisting entirely of Australian-born ministers.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Yet Bruce himself was frequently caricatured in public as "an Englishman who happened to have been born in Australia".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography_20-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> He drove a <a href="/wiki/Rolls-Royce_Limited" title="Rolls-Royce Limited">Rolls-Royce</a>, wore white <a href="/wiki/Spats_(footwear)" title="Spats (footwear)">spats</a>, and was often seen as distant and lacking the common touch: characteristics that did little to personally endear him to the Australian public.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography_20-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id=".22Men.2C_money_and_markets.22"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="&quot;Men,_money_and_markets&quot;">"Men, money and markets"</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: &quot;Men, money and markets&quot;">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Australian_Migration_Poster,_1928.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Australian_Migration_Poster%2C_1928.jpg/220px-Australian_Migration_Poster%2C_1928.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="335" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Australian_Migration_Poster%2C_1928.jpg/330px-Australian_Migration_Poster%2C_1928.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Australian_Migration_Poster%2C_1928.jpg/440px-Australian_Migration_Poster%2C_1928.jpg 2x" data-file-width="722" data-file-height="1100" /></a><figcaption>Poster promoting migration to Australia as part of the "men, money and markets" scheme, 1928</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1923 Australia was prosperous by comparison with other developed nations of the period, having quickly rebounded economically after World War I. Unemployment and inflation were relatively low by international standards, and <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Commonwealth of Australia">Commonwealth</a> revenues had grown significantly since Australia became a federation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards84_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards84-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Australia was a vast and richly resourced country with fewer than six million inhabitants, and Bruce made it his government's priority to develop Australia's economy. In his first speech to the House of Representatives as prime minister, he outlined a comprehensive vision for Australia that centred on economic development, reform of the <a href="/wiki/Federalism_in_Australia" title="Federalism in Australia">federal system</a>, increased Commonwealth powers over industrial relations, a greater voice for Australia within the British Empire and the establishment of a national capital. He summarised this vision as a program of "men, money and markets".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENational_Archives_of_AustraliaIn_Office_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENational_Archives_of_AustraliaIn_Office-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to Bruce, men were needed to allow Australia's extensive resources to be developed. In 1923, much of Australia's land was virtually unoccupied, and Bruce believed Australia had the potential to be one of the most fertile and productive nations in the world, which could sustain populations upward of 100&#160;million over time&#160;&#8211;&#32; more than 16 times the population of his time.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee38_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee38-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> Despite dissenting voices from scientists, who noted that poor climate, soils and water availability were significant barriers to large populations, the Bruce-Page government enacted policies to encourage large numbers of British to migrate to Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee38_65-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee38-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> Under the auspices of the new Development and Migration Commission, £34&#160;million in loans took place over the decade starting in 1924 to facilitate immigrant settlement through improvements to rural infrastructure, land access, and subsidising immigrant journeys ("passages").<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Estimates as high as half a million British immigrants over ten years were predicted at the start of the policy, whereas just over 200,000 travelled to Australia during that time period.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMacintyre168_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacintyre168-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Bruce's settlement plan rested on rural growth. Migrants were often selected on the basis of their willingness to work on the land; state and Commonwealth governments concentrated their investment on rural development and encouraged returned servicemen to take up farms on the periphery of settled areas.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMacintyre168–169_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacintyre168–169-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> Despite this, a majority of these migrants settled in urban areas, as Australia's rural areas were far more remote and difficult to work (than for example England's) and many of those taking advantage of the assistance scheme were urban workers or family and friends of those already settled.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
Immigration from outside Great Britain and her dominions was considered unpalatable&#160;&#8211;&#32; the Bruce government upheld the <a href="/wiki/White_Australia_policy" title="White Australia policy">White Australia policy</a> by placing strong restrictions on immigration from other areas, notwithstanding its population growth targets.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMacintyre125–127_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacintyre125–127-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> In his campaign speech for the 1925 election, Bruce stated:<sup id="cite_ref-Policy_Launch_Speech_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Policy_Launch_Speech-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup></p><blockquote><p>It is necessary that we should determine what are the ideals towards which every Australian would desire to strive. I think those ideals might well be stated as being to secure our national safety, and to ensure the maintenance of our White Australia Policy to continue as an integral portion of the British Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-Policy_Launch_Speech_71-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Policy_Launch_Speech-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> We intend to keep this country white and not allow its people to be faced with the problems that at present are practically insoluble in many parts of the world.<sup id="cite_ref-Bowen_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bowen-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote><p>
Money was borrowed from Britain to fund the state's programs and at an unprecedented rate. Over £230&#160;million was extended in loans from the <a href="/wiki/City_of_London" title="City of London">City of London</a> to state and Commonwealth treasuries during the 1920s. A further £140&#160;million arrived through private investment.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMacintyre168_67-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacintyre168-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Bruce's plan for Australian economic development needed a much stronger role for the Commonwealth government than had been traditionally accepted. Both he and Page were "conspicuously national rather than federal in their outlook"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENethercote126_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENethercote126-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> and sought major changes to federal-state relations in order implement their development policy. </p><blockquote><p>Increasingly our problems are becoming national in character&#160;... Our financial resources are curtailed and there is an immediate repercussion throughout the Commonwealth. All our problems are common problems. None can be prosperous unless all are prospering. I am more convinced that we have to look at all our problems with the eyes of a nation and not as individuals. Where a great problem confronts a State it may be solved by the co-operation of the Commonwealth for the benefit of the States, and the benefit and advancement of the whole of Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>The Bruce–Page plan of May 1923 put in motion efforts to coordinate state-federal operations in several areas, particularly infrastructure and rural development schemes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee35–36_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee35–36-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> The <i>Main Roads Development Act of 1923</i> was one of the first and most important legislative accomplishments in this vein. The act leveraged Section 96 of the <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Australia" title="Constitution of Australia">Constitution of Australia</a> to grant financial assistance to the states by employing it to fund road construction and maintenance according to the plans of the federal transportation portfolio&#160;&#8211;&#32; in effect allowing the Commonwealth to operate directly in what was constitutionality the exclusive domain of the state governments. The Act would provide a precedent for many types of "special purpose payments" that became a common feature of Australian federal fiscal relations.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Despite some major successes, Bruce was more frequently frustrated by a lack of progress in many key areas of intergovernmental co-operation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography_20-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The states could not be induced to standardise electrical power schemes, nor unify on <a href="/wiki/Track_gauge" title="Track gauge">track gauges</a>, nor <a href="/wiki/National_health_insurance" title="National health insurance">national health insurance</a> despite years of work and solid arguments in favour.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards94–95_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards94–95-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:StanleyBruce2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/StanleyBruce2.jpg/220px-StanleyBruce2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="311" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/StanleyBruce2.jpg/330px-StanleyBruce2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/d0/StanleyBruce2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="402" data-file-height="568" /></a><figcaption>Bruce in about 1925</figcaption></figure>
<p>Although men and money had been secured, the markets component of the Bruce plan was never fully realised. At the <a href="/wiki/1923_Imperial_Conference" title="1923 Imperial Conference">1923 Imperial Conference</a>, Bruce lobbied consistently for the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative</a> government of <a href="/wiki/Stanley_Baldwin" title="Stanley Baldwin">Stanley Baldwin</a> to make changes to Great Britain's trading arrangements to give <a href="/wiki/Trade_preference" title="Trade preference">preference</a> to dominion products over imports from other nations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett81–83_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett81–83-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> He argued for Empire-wide economic trading arrangements that filled domestic demands by production from member states before seeking supplemental imports from other countries and empires. Baldwin and the Conservatives attempted to introduce such a scheme in Britain; however, the British public feared higher prices for basic products (particularly food), and this fear was a factor in the Conservative government's defeat in the <a href="/wiki/1923_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1923 United Kingdom general election">December 1923 election</a>. Baldwin's successor <a href="/wiki/Ramsay_MacDonald" title="Ramsay MacDonald">Ramsay MacDonald</a> repudiated the plan, much to Bruce's chagrin, and attempts to revive negotiations foundered as economic conditions worsened throughout the decade.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee40_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee40-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> World agricultural prices stalled in the mid-1920s as European and American agricultural production recovered to pre-war levels, and Australian exports were crowded out of markets as the decade progressed.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1927, Earle Page handed down the first budget in deficit for the coalition government, and Bruce recognised that Australia's economic position was deteriorating. Federal and state debt that year totalled just over £1&#160;billion, of which £305&#160;million were war debts and the rest had been spent on development that had failed had deliver high returns.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston74_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston74-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Nearly half of the total debt was owed to overseas lenders, principally those in London. Economic growth was slow, and far lower than the levels hoped. Exports and revenues were falling behind government needs, and investors had begun to express alarm at Australia's level of debt.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> Bruce persisted with his plans and believed that growing Australian exports were the key to rectifying the problems, thus justifying further investment and encouragement of population growth.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston75_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston75-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> The government did act to try to manage the debt problem. By the mid-1920s, states were borrowing at unsustainable rates to fund their own programs to compensate for dwindling revenues. In response, Bruce proposed that the responsibility for all government debts, Commonwealth and state, and the authority to acquire new debt, should be handed over to a <a href="/wiki/Loan_Council" title="Loan Council">National Loan Council</a> in which all states would have one vote and the Commonwealth would have two votes and the casting vote.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESawer131–132_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESawer131–132-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> He also moved to abolish per capita payments to the states, to be replaced by a funding formula tied more to financial need. These two changes formed the <i>Financial Agreement of 1927</i>, the provisions of which were approved by <a href="/wiki/1928_Australian_referendum" class="mw-redirect" title="1928 Australian referendum">referendum in 1928</a>. These changes would prove to be amongst the most significant in Australian constitutional history, as the states had now lost much of their financial independence.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESawer131–132_85-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESawer131–132-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMathews9–19_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMathews9–19-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Faced with severe financial pressures and an increasing reliance upon Commonwealth transfer payments, after some resistance the states consented, although vertical fiscal imbalance between the states and the Commonwealth continued to be an enduring feature of Australian federal relations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlexander69–70_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander69–70-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Modernising_government">Modernising government</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Modernising government">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bruce_opens_Parliament_House,_Canberra.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Bruce_opens_Parliament_House%2C_Canberra.jpg/220px-Bruce_opens_Parliament_House%2C_Canberra.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="177" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Bruce_opens_Parliament_House%2C_Canberra.jpg/330px-Bruce_opens_Parliament_House%2C_Canberra.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/a3/Bruce_opens_Parliament_House%2C_Canberra.jpg 2x" data-file-width="425" data-file-height="341" /></a><figcaption>Bruce speaking at the opening of Parliament House, Canberra on 9 May 1927</figcaption></figure>
<p>Bruce set about applying his business principles to his cabinet, putting his experience as both a corporate manager and rowing coach to use in a cabinet system that was orderly and practical.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHawkins77_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHawkins77-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeller76–77_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeller76–77-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> He implemented a formal system whereby a proper agenda for cabinet meetings would be formulated, and the minister responsible for each item would circulate papers to bring other members up to speed on the issue. His decision-making procedures ensured that his colleagues were informed and actively participated in decisions&#160;&#8211;&#32; or if they disagreed, gave leave for cabinet members to absent themselves from meetings to preserve cabinet solidarity.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeller53–55_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeller53–55-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> In this respect Bruce earned the quick respect and approval of his colleagues, and in many respects came to dominate cabinet through his industriousness and knowledgability.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMurray127_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurray127-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> Cabinet minister George Pearce would later conclude that Bruce was the best of the prime ministers that he served or opposed in his 38-year parliamentary career.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston29_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston29-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bruce also greatly strengthened the research and information-gathering capacity of the executive, and sought to make decisions and policy on the basis of the best available evidence and information. A record 22 <a href="/wiki/Royal_Commission" class="mw-redirect" title="Royal Commission">Royal Commissions</a> reported to the Bruce government, as well as scores of other inquiries and research projects undertaken by the Commonwealth in a wide range of areas, particularly on economic, industrial and agricultural matters. Recognising that investment in science was essential to expanding the opportunities for agricultural and economic development in Australia, Bruce established the <a href="/wiki/Council_for_Scientific_and_Industrial_Research" title="Council for Scientific and Industrial Research">Council for Scientific and Industrial Research</a> (CSIR), now known as the CSIRO.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards126_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards126-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> He established the Bureau of Economic Research in 1929 and for the first time economists were regularly employed to advise the government on what had been until then exclusively political issues.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHawkins80_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHawkins80-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMillmow147–148_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillmow147–148-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> By the time he left office, he had put in place extensive structures to provide information to the prime minister and the executive branch on most major areas of policy, a major development in the professional capacity of the Australian prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeller56–76_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeller56–76-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Nla_Bruce_thelodge.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Nla_Bruce_thelodge.jpg/220px-Nla_Bruce_thelodge.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="163" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Nla_Bruce_thelodge.jpg/330px-Nla_Bruce_thelodge.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Nla_Bruce_thelodge.jpg/440px-Nla_Bruce_thelodge.jpg 2x" data-file-width="840" data-file-height="623" /></a><figcaption>Stanley and Ethel Bruce after moving into <a href="/wiki/The_Lodge_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="The Lodge (Australia)">The Lodge</a>, 1926</figcaption></figure>
<p>Under Bruce the Australian government also moved to its new permanent home in the planned federal city of <a href="/wiki/Canberra" title="Canberra">Canberra</a>. Plans for a new federal capital had been circulated since the time of Federation, and a site on the <a href="/wiki/Molonglo_River" title="Molonglo River">Molonglo River</a> was selected in 1913. Design and construction had proceeded slowly due to World War I as well as funding and management problems. Bruce was a committed advocate of the new capital, however, stating: "For the purposes of Federation it was essential that there should be some central point of nationhood, suitable to Australia as a nation&#160;... and sooner or later there would have to be a national capital, overriding State boundaries and State prejudices."<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> This came to pass on 9 May 1927 when the <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Australia" title="Parliament of Australia">Federal Parliament</a> moved to its new premises in Canberra; Bruce had moved himself and his wife to <a href="/wiki/The_Lodge_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="The Lodge (Australia)">The Lodge</a> a few days earlier.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> At the Federal Parliament's opening ceremony, Bruce decided that voices of the clerks of the House of Representatives and the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Senate" title="Australian Senate">Senate</a> were too heavily <a href="/wiki/Australian_accent" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian accent">accented</a>, and determined that he would personally welcome the <a href="/wiki/George_VI" title="George VI">Duke of York</a> and co-open the new building&#160;&#8211;&#32; a move that was criticised by several parliamentarians.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee69_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee69-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> The business of transferring government and cabinet operations to the new capital proceeded slowly over 1927 and 1928. This was an administrative task that occupied a significant proportion of the government's time in those years, with many departments only very gradually transferring from Melbourne, much to Bruce's consternation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston76–77_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston76–77-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Imperial_overtures">Imperial overtures</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Imperial overtures">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Imperial_Conference_1923_Cartoon.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Imperial_Conference_1923_Cartoon.png/350px-Imperial_Conference_1923_Cartoon.png" decoding="async" width="350" height="277" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Imperial_Conference_1923_Cartoon.png/525px-Imperial_Conference_1923_Cartoon.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Imperial_Conference_1923_Cartoon.png/700px-Imperial_Conference_1923_Cartoon.png 2x" data-file-width="818" data-file-height="648" /></a><figcaption>Much coverage was given to the stand taken by Bruce at the 1923 Imperial Conference.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Loyal to the concept of the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a>, Bruce envisaged strengthening it through mutual economic development, greater political co-operation, and common policies on defence, trade and foreign affairs. His first trip abroad as prime minister was for the 1923 Imperial Conference in London. Bruce put forward a wide range of proposals for stronger co-operation between Great Britain and her <a href="/wiki/Dominion" title="Dominion">dominions</a> in a wide range of areas, particularly in the areas of trade, defence and the formulation of a common foreign policy. He particularly desired a greater role for the dominions in imperial affairs, including a collective decision-making body for common questions of defence and foreign affairs. He took exception to a lack of involvement in foreign policy decisions made by Britain that would have significant ramifications for Australia. In 1922, in what became known as the <a href="/wiki/Chanak_Crisis" title="Chanak Crisis">Chanak Crisis</a>, British brinkmanship over Turkey's aggressive manoeuvres to <a href="/wiki/Greco-Turkish_War_(1919%E2%80%9322)" class="mw-redirect" title="Greco-Turkish War (1919–22)">redefine its border with Greece</a> had escalated to the point where the British cabinet had threatened war against Turkey. This threat included military participation of dominions in an intervention, though no dominion government had been informed of the developments in Turkey.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> Although a diplomatic end to the crisis was eventually found, dominion leaders were angered at having almost been committed to a military conflict without any consultation.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bruce pushed for greater consultation and voice for the dominions, and succeeded in having <a href="/wiki/Richard_Casey,_Baron_Casey" title="Richard Casey, Baron Casey">Richard Casey</a> appointed as a permanent political liaison in London who would have access to British government decisions and act as a conduit between Westminster and Melbourne.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndrews48–51_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrews48–51-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> He also successfully lobbied for the creation of a <a href="/wiki/Dominion_Office" class="mw-redirect" title="Dominion Office">Dominion Office</a> separated out from the <a href="/wiki/Colonial_Office" title="Colonial Office">Colonial Office</a> to acknowledge the different state of affairs that now existed in the Empire. However, while fellow dominions generally agreed that the imperial system should be modified, not all shared his vision for closer collaboration and strong ties. Canada and the <a href="/wiki/Union_of_South_Africa" title="Union of South Africa">Union of South Africa</a> were instead seeking greater independence from London, and there was little enthusiasm for many of Bruce's more ambitious plans for inter-empire trade and policy co-operation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAndrews50–51_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrews50–51-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite Australia's greater representation in London after 1923, Bruce's hope for collective imperial decision-making was dashed once more with the British decision to recognise the Soviet Union in 1924. This act dismayed the fervently anti-communist Bruce, who disagreed with the decision ideologically and on the grounds that once again the dominions had not been consulted.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> Ultimately, though, the differences between Britain's and her dominions' opinions on the matter were too great to be reconciled. Instead, Britain signed the <i>General Treaty</i> with the Soviet Union only on her own behalf, marking the first major split between Britain and Australia on a matter of foreign policy.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> Despite its applicability solely to European security arrangements, Bruce had criticised Britain's signing of the <a href="/wiki/Locarno_Treaties" title="Locarno Treaties">Locarno Treaties</a> in 1925 without dominion consultation;<sup id="cite_ref-trove_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-trove-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> though the eventual treaty was not binding on the dominions.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ImperialConference.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/ImperialConference.jpg/220px-ImperialConference.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="175" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/ImperialConference.jpg/330px-ImperialConference.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/ImperialConference.jpg/440px-ImperialConference.jpg 2x" data-file-width="635" data-file-height="506" /></a><figcaption>Bruce (Back row centre) at the <a href="/wiki/1926_Imperial_Conference" title="1926 Imperial Conference">1926 Imperial Conference</a></figcaption></figure><p>The <a href="/wiki/1926_Imperial_Conference" title="1926 Imperial Conference">1926 Imperial Conference</a> confirmed that Britain and her dominions were rapidly diverging in interests and that greater independence&#160;&#8211;&#32; not greater co-operation&#160;&#8211;&#32; was the most practical solution. Bruce recognised the empire had reached a turning point, but despite his optimism for a reinvigorated imperial system, there was little interest from other governments.<sup id="cite_ref-trove_107-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-trove-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> His plan for greater economic and defensive co-operation was discussed, as well as a revival of imperial preference in trading arrangements, but such ideas were now politically unpalatable in Britain and failed to develop.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston70–73_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston70–73-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> His attitudes were almost the opposite of those held by the leaders of Canada, South Africa and the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Free_State" title="Irish Free State">Irish Free State</a>. As Prime Minister Baldwin remarked at the conference, "If you, Mr Bruce, would use the word 'Empire' a bit less and you <a href="/wiki/Patrick_McGilligan_(Fine_Gael_politician)" title="Patrick McGilligan (Fine Gael politician)">Mr McGilligan</a> would use it a bit more, then we would make better progress."<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> Ultimately proposals for greater independence won out, and 1926 Imperial Conference recognised through the <a href="/wiki/Balfour_Declaration_of_1926" title="Balfour Declaration of 1926">Balfour Declaration</a> that dominions were essentially independent entities that freely associated as the <a href="/wiki/British_Commonwealth_of_Nations" class="mw-redirect" title="British Commonwealth of Nations">British Commonwealth of Nations</a>, though after much negotiation it stopped short of using the terms <i>nation</i> or <i>state</i>. <a href="/wiki/Governor-General" class="mw-redirect" title="Governor-General">Governors-General</a> were redefined as representatives only of the <a href="/wiki/British_monarch" class="mw-redirect" title="British monarch">British monarch</a>, not the <a href="/wiki/British_government" class="mw-redirect" title="British government">British government</a>, and were obliged now to act only on the advice of their respective dominion governments. These changes were both symbolically and practically significant in the transformation of the dominions from colonies to independent nations.<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> Bruce had mixed feelings about these developments; on the one hand he believed that the Empire was still a strong and vital international organisation playing a positive role in international affairs, but on the other hand he was disappointed that other member nations did not share his vision or commitment to maintaining its integrity and unity.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston74_81-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston74-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Bruce did have some successes at the conference, however, expanding the work of the Imperial Economic Committee and increasing inter-empire co-operation in technical and scientific areas.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston72_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston72-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p><h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Industrial_relations">Industrial relations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Industrial relations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:1929_Timberworkers_Strike.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/1929_Timberworkers_Strike.jpg/220px-1929_Timberworkers_Strike.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="182" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/1929_Timberworkers_Strike.jpg/330px-1929_Timberworkers_Strike.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/1929_Timberworkers_Strike.jpg/440px-1929_Timberworkers_Strike.jpg 2x" data-file-width="582" data-file-height="482" /></a><figcaption>Workers protesting against the decisions of the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Court_of_Conciliation_and_Arbitration" title="Commonwealth Court of Conciliation and Arbitration">Commonwealth Court of Conciliation and Arbitration</a> during the <a href="/wiki/1929_Timber_Workers_strike" class="mw-redirect" title="1929 Timber Workers strike">1929 Timber Workers strike</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Strikes and industrial unrest were frequent in the aftermath of World War I, arising from discontent over poor conditions and the rise of militant labour organisations like the <a href="/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Australia" title="Communist Party of Australia">Communist Party of Australia</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Industrial_Workers_of_the_World" title="Industrial Workers of the World">Industrial Workers of the World</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPlowman50–55_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlowman50–55-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> The problems were made worse in Australia due to the overlapping system of industrial courts that had evolved since Federation.<sup id="cite_ref-Australian_Parliamentary_Library_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Australian_Parliamentary_Library-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> Although <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Court_of_Conciliation_and_Arbitration" title="Commonwealth Court of Conciliation and
