Arbitration">federal arbitration</a> was intended for industrial disputes that impacted upon multiple states or federal employees, courts had through several judgments greatly expanded the potential jurisdiction of federal arbitration, and the system had developed into a duality with both state and federal tribunals weighing into a wide range of industrial disputes.<sup id="cite_ref-Australian_Parliamentary_Library_114-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Australian_Parliamentary_Library-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> This was exploited by both unions and employers, who pursued matters in the courts they felt most likely to be favourable and switched between different jurisdictions to arrive at the best combination of state and federal conditions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPlowman49–54_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlowman49–54-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> Protracted disputes arose as employers and employees pursued disputes in different courts or refused to recognise the rulings of one in favour of the other. Billy Hughes and several of his predecessors had sought resolution to the problem through greater Commonwealth powers, but all <a href="/wiki/Referendums_in_Australia" title="Referendums in Australia">referendums</a> to expand Commonwealth industrial powers had failed at the ballot box.<sup id="cite_ref-Australian_Parliamentary_Library_114-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Australian_Parliamentary_Library-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bruce's attitudes towards industrial relations were varied, and he was initially unsympathetic to either employer or employee complaints, believing the best resolutions to be those brokered between businesses and their employees. He frequently called upon both sides to embrace a spirit of co-operation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography_20-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The situation became acute when waterfront workers led by the <a href="/wiki/Seamen%27s_Union_of_Australia" title="Seamen&#39;s Union of Australia">Seamen's Union</a> went on strike in 1925. This had severe and immediate effect on the Australian economy, reliant as it was on sea transportation for its imports and exports, a situation the Prime Minister recognised as untenable.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> With the union disregarding Commonwealth rulings on the dispute, Bruce rushed through the <i>Navigation Act</i> and the <i>Immigration Act</i>. The first allowed British and foreign shipping not working under Australian industrial awards to operate in Australian waters (although British vessels working in Australia would soon initiate a strike of their own). The second allowed the Commonwealth to deport any foreign-born person whom a special tribunal found guilty of "disrupting the industrial life of the community".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards112_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards112-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee51–52_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee51–52-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>  Key foreign-born strike leaders were immediately targeted for deportation, but New South Wales Premier <a href="/wiki/Jack_Lang_(Australian_politician)" title="Jack Lang (Australian politician)">Jack Lang</a> refused to allow <a href="/wiki/New_South_Wales_Police_Force" title="New South Wales Police Force">state police</a> to serve summonses on two leading union leaders. Bruce responded with the <i>Peace Officers Act</i>, which re-established a <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Police" title="Commonwealth Police">Commonwealth police force</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards112–114_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards112–114-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Stanley_Bruce_-_Reynolds.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Stanley_Bruce_-_Reynolds.jpg/220px-Stanley_Bruce_-_Reynolds.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="348" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Stanley_Bruce_-_Reynolds.jpg/330px-Stanley_Bruce_-_Reynolds.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/94/Stanley_Bruce_-_Reynolds.jpg/440px-Stanley_Bruce_-_Reynolds.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="1581" /></a><figcaption>Caricature of Bruce as prime minister</figcaption></figure>
<p>These heavy-handed tactics drew outrage from the Labor Party, which challenged the Prime Minister to seek approval from the people. This Bruce did, and the <a href="/wiki/1925_Australian_federal_election" title="1925 Australian federal election">elections of 1925</a> were Australia's first "<a href="/wiki/Red_scare" class="mw-redirect" title="Red scare">red scare</a>" election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESouter204_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESouter204-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> He campaigned for industrial peace and an increased Commonwealth role in securing it, but also denounced "foreign agitators" and "class war" in appealing for law and order on the waterfront.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography_20-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> He openly challenged pre-existing federal arrangements, stating on the campaign trail that Australia "should now consider whether that great historic instrument, the Constitution, meets the needs of to-day in the light of the developments which have taken place".<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> The campaign was a success and the Bruce government was easily re-elected, increasing by 11 seats its majority over a disheartened ALP, whose leader <a href="/wiki/Matthew_Charlton" title="Matthew Charlton">Matthew Charlton</a> was in poor health.
</p><p>"My government was returned to power on a clear issue and with a definite mission&#160;... to introduce measures for the preservation of industrial peace", Bruce declared during his second term,<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> and called <a href="/wiki/1926_Australian_referendum" title="1926 Australian referendum">a referendum</a> to amend the Australian Constitution and bring industrial relations under the exclusive jurisdiction of the Commonwealth. Attempts to amend the constitution for this purpose had been rejected narrowly in <a href="/wiki/Australian_referendum,_1911_(Trade_and_Commerce)" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian referendum, 1911 (Trade and Commerce)">1911</a>, <a href="/wiki/Australian_referendum,_1913_(Industrial_Matters)" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian referendum, 1913 (Industrial Matters)">1913</a> and <a href="/wiki/Australian_referendum,_1919_(Legislative_Powers)" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian referendum, 1919 (Legislative Powers)">1919</a>, but against a background of ongoing industrial strife, Bruce made clear the problems of mixed jurisdiction, confusing dual-awards, and insufficient regulatory and enforcement powers at the Commonwealth level.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWildavsky12–14_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWildavsky12–14-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> Controversially, he also formulated an "essential services" question to be put at the same time, giving the government the power to protect the public from any "actual or probable disruption to essential services". These proposals drew hostile reception from many quarters&#160;&#8211;&#32; even from future conservative prime minister <a href="/wiki/Robert_Menzies" title="Robert Menzies">Robert Menzies</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32; and both the Australian Labor Party and conservative groups were divided in opinion on the amendments, with the ALP eventually taking no fixed position on the questions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWildavsky10–24_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWildavsky10–24-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> But by September the issue had drawn so much controversy and ill-feeling that it was no surprise for many commentators that the proposals failed to attract sufficient popular support to overcome the high bar for constitutional change.<sup id="cite_ref-Australian_Parliamentary_Library_114-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Australian_Parliamentary_Library-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Dog_Collar_Waterfront_Licence,_1929.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Dog_Collar_Waterfront_Licence%2C_1929.jpg/220px-Dog_Collar_Waterfront_Licence%2C_1929.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="94" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Dog_Collar_Waterfront_Licence%2C_1929.jpg/330px-Dog_Collar_Waterfront_Licence%2C_1929.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Dog_Collar_Waterfront_Licence%2C_1929.jpg/440px-Dog_Collar_Waterfront_Licence%2C_1929.jpg 2x" data-file-width="598" data-file-height="255" /></a><figcaption>A license or 'dog collar' issued to a waterfront worker under the Transport Workers Act in 1929</figcaption></figure>
<p>The problems of industrial unrest continued to flare up, and the waterfront remained a flashpoint. In a review of the Commonwealth Conciliation and Arbitration Act in 1928, Bruce required industrial courts to consider the economic effects of its awards in addition to the welfare of worker.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography_20-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Immediate problems followed when a new award for waterside workers in 1928 worsened conditions for workers on economic grounds. New industrial action led by the <a href="/wiki/Maritime_Union_of_Australia" title="Maritime Union of Australia">Waterside Workers Federation</a> broke out. In Melbourne these turned to riots, claiming casualties and at least one fatality. Reviving the "red scare" pitch for the <a href="/wiki/1928_Australian_federal_election" title="1928 Australian federal election">1928 election</a> campaign, Bruce pushed the <i><a href="/wiki/Transport_Workers_Act_1928" title="Transport Workers Act 1928">Transport Workers Act</a></i> through parliament in response, which gave the executive unprecedented regulatory power in industrial relations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESawer265_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESawer265-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> All waterfront workers now required federal licenses, or "dog collars" as they were derisively known, to work.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee79_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee79-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> The act allowed the Commonwealth government to effectively control who worked on the docks and nearly destroyed the Waterside Workers Federation, earning the government deep unpopularity among organised labour.
</p><p>At the 1928 election Bruce's government was returned to power, but it now had a majority of just nine&#160;&#8211;&#32; a fragile result, given the growing propensity for Nationalist member defections.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarber29_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarber29-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMurray119_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurray119-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> Most of the Nationalists' parliamentary gains from 1925 had been wiped out. <a href="/wiki/James_Scullin" title="James Scullin">James Scullin</a> had assumed the leadership of the ALP earlier in the year, upon the retirement of Charlton, and the party was invigorated as a consequence. Bruce was undeterred, though, and became convinced that unless costs of production for industry in Australia could be lowered and industrial peace secured, Australia was heading towards a major economic crisis. This doctrine was the basis of his appeals to the public for the next year and a half.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Museum_of_Australian_Democracy_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Museum_of_Australian_Democracy-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Maritime_industries_crisis">Maritime industries crisis</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Maritime industries crisis">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Waterside_Workers_of_Australia_Banner.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Waterside_Workers_of_Australia_Banner.jpg/220px-Waterside_Workers_of_Australia_Banner.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="247" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Waterside_Workers_of_Australia_Banner.jpg/330px-Waterside_Workers_of_Australia_Banner.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Waterside_Workers_of_Australia_Banner.jpg/440px-Waterside_Workers_of_Australia_Banner.jpg 2x" data-file-width="463" data-file-height="519" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Maritime_Union_of_Australia" title="Maritime Union of Australia">Waterfront Workers Federation</a> was highly militant during the 1920s. Much of Bruce's industrial legislation was targeted at curbing the organisation's power.</figcaption></figure>
<p>As early as 1927 economic indicators were signalling the onset of a recession in Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchedvin40–48_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchedvin40–48-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> By 1929, the evidence was unavoidable that recession was becoming depression as a resulting of rapidly deteriorating international conditions. In that year, prices for Australian exports fell by nearly a third and GDP had fallen by 10%, with no attendant fall in the cost of living.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee77_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee77-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> Australia's debt now stood at £631&#160;million&#160;&#8211;&#32; the interest payments on which were equivalent to nearly half of Australia's export receipts, a situation difficult to sustain even in a positive economic climate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMillmowChapter_3_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillmowChapter_3-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> Investors and banks recognised the risk and Australia's credit access was rapidly drying up. Bruce's grand economic development plans had increased the national debt significantly yet had delivered only marginal economic expansion in the short term.
</p><p>To make matters worse for the government, industrial unrest was a persisting problem, particularly in the economically key states of New South Wales and Victoria.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee77_132-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee77-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> The opposition had become more potent as well: Scullin was widely recognised as one of the parliament's most gifted and articulate members; even his opponents respected his personal probity. Scullin forecast that the Australian economy was highly vulnerable given its indebtedness, and was on a course for severe depression if government finances were not turned around dramatically.<sup id="cite_ref-Scullin,_James_Henry_1876–1953_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Scullin,_James_Henry_1876–1953-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Furthermore, the Prime Minister's own party was breaking down underneath him. There had been no official party platform yet agreed to, and individual members had a wide degree of latitude to determine the issues they ran on, leading to frequent government defections on legislation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStarr20–21_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStarr20–21-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> The government's existing nine-seat majority shrank during 1928 and 1929 over various issues, as a result of by-elections, and several leading figures who had previously worked with Bruce became increasingly sharp critics of him. Hughes and <a href="/wiki/Edward_Mann_(Australian_politician)" title="Edward Mann (Australian politician)">Edward Mann</a> were two of the government's most ardent opponents in 1929.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESouter248–249_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESouter248–249-136">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Worsening economic conditions had put further pressure on industrial relations, with unemployment rising and employers seeking to cut costs. Strikes of sugar mill workers in 1927, waterside workers in 1928, and then transport and timber industry workers in 1929 continued to disrupt economic operations. The worst came with ongoing disputes between miners and mine owners on the coalfields of New South Wales had culminated in riots and lockouts in 1929, and intervention by the Bruce government failed to produce a settlement. Notably, in March 1929 <a href="/wiki/Newcastle,_New_South_Wales" title="Newcastle, New South Wales">Newcastle</a>-based mine owner John Brown locked out workers from his mines at <a href="/wiki/Pelaw_Main,_New_South_Wales" title="Pelaw Main, New South Wales">Pelaw Main</a> and Richmond Main to try to force acceptance of lower wages.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> Attorney-General <a href="/wiki/John_Latham_(judge)" title="John Latham (judge)">John Latham</a> pursued legal action against Brown for illegally using coercive industrial tactics, but Bruce intervened to stop the prosecution, believing the case to be doubtful and that dropping it would give the government a chance to negotiate an end to the dispute. The mine-owners refused to accede to government-brokered negotiations anyway, and the government was seen to have been siding with rich businesses and applying double standards, undermining Bruce's own credibility as an impartial leader and law-and-order prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee83–85_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee83–85-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Stanley_Bruce_and_John_Latham.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/Stanley_Bruce_and_John_Latham.jpg/220px-Stanley_Bruce_and_John_Latham.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="289" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/Stanley_Bruce_and_John_Latham.jpg/330px-Stanley_Bruce_and_John_Latham.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/Stanley_Bruce_and_John_Latham.jpg/440px-Stanley_Bruce_and_John_Latham.jpg 2x" data-file-width="519" data-file-height="681" /></a><figcaption>Bruce with <a href="/wiki/John_Latham_(judge)" title="John Latham (judge)">John Latham</a>, his Attorney-General and eventual successor as leader of the Nationalist Party</figcaption></figure>
<p>Bruce and the conservatives in parliament became increasingly convinced that "economic deterioration [was] the product, rather than the possible cause, of worsening industrial relations."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPlowman54_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlowman54-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> Exasperated, the Prime Minister made a dramatic move and presented an ultimatum to the Australian state governments: either they should voluntarily hand their powers of industrial regulation to the federal government, or the federal government would divest itself of its industrial powers and dismantle federal arbitration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee85_140-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee85-140">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> The announcement came as a major shock to all sides of politics, not least the members of Bruce's own cabinet, most of whom had not been informed of the dramatic change in policy until the day it was announced to the states.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards171–172_141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards171–172-141">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> Bruce calculated that the states would not give up their powers, and thus the move was one designed to sanction the end of Commonwealth arbitration. For Bruce, the only inconceivable outcome now was that the status quo of dual jurisdiction remained unchanged.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> The opposition to the proposal, which the Prime Minister introduced to the parliament as the <i>Maritime Industries Bill</i>, was swift and fierce. In 1929 over 150 unions and 700,000 workers were covered by Commonwealth awards over a wide range of industries&#160;&#8211;&#32; although there was dissatisfaction in the coal and timber sectors, most were happy with their arrangements and feared worse pay and conditions if moved back to state awards.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee86_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee86-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> He defended his actions as being necessary to create certainty and end the duplication that had caused so many problems in recent years.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECommonwealth_Parliamentary_DebatesVol._121,_p._862_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECommonwealth_Parliamentary_DebatesVol._121,_p._862-144">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Other government decisions in 1929 had attracted controversy as well. Page handed down his last budget on 22 August, which hinted at the burgeoning debt crisis and introduced a new tax on entertainment and theatres to help cover the deficit.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee86_143-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee86-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> The tax was very unpopular with the public, and the entertainment industry mounted a vocal campaign to stop it. Bruce, supported by the <i>Brigden Report</i> of 1929, also raised concern that protective tariffs were too high and were in need of reform&#160;&#8211;&#32; a position controversial with the powerful protected industries.<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The second reading of the <i>Maritime Industries Bill</i> passed by only four votes, with Hughes, Edward Mann and <a href="/wiki/George_Maxwell_(Australian_politician)" title="George Maxwell (Australian politician)">George Maxwell</a> voting against the government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESouter249–252_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESouter249–252-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Hughes moved an amendment to the bill that stipulated it should only take effect after being approved by the people at a referendum or general election. Bruce ruled out any referendum, stating that the amendment would constitute a vote of confidence in his government and urged his party to vote it down.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESouter249–252_146-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESouter249–252-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Nationalist MP <a href="/wiki/Walter_Marks_(politician)" title="Walter Marks (politician)">Walter Marks</a> and Independent MP <a href="/wiki/William_McWilliams" title="William McWilliams">William McWilliams</a> joined the opposition, giving the opposition a one-vote majority. Bruce and his supporters now lobbied the Speaker, Sir <a href="/wiki/Littleton_Groom" title="Littleton Groom">Littleton Groom</a> to make a deliberative vote in committee to tie the numbers, leaving the Committee chairman <a href="/wiki/James_Bayley_(politician)" title="James Bayley (politician)">James Bayley</a> with the casting (and presumably pro-government) vote. Groom refused, citing long-standing <a href="/wiki/British_Parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="British Parliament">Westminster</a> (though not Australian) parliamentary tradition of the speaker's impartiality. Groom's bitterness at having been dismissed by Bruce from the job of <a href="/wiki/Attorney-General_of_Australia" title="Attorney-General of Australia">Attorney-General</a> in 1925 had a significant bearing on his later decision.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESouter251–252_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESouter251–252-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bruce_resigned_newPMScullin_brief_meeting.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Bruce_resigned_newPMScullin_brief_meeting.jpg/220px-Bruce_resigned_newPMScullin_brief_meeting.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="170" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Bruce_resigned_newPMScullin_brief_meeting.jpg/330px-Bruce_resigned_newPMScullin_brief_meeting.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Bruce_resigned_newPMScullin_brief_meeting.jpg/440px-Bruce_resigned_newPMScullin_brief_meeting.jpg 2x" data-file-width="750" data-file-height="579" /></a><figcaption>Bruce meeting with Prime Minister-elect <a href="/wiki/James_Scullin" title="James Scullin">Scullin</a> a day before Scullin's swearing in</figcaption></figure>
<p>A snap election was called, with Bruce taking his case to the people that dramatic action on industrial relations was needed.<sup id="cite_ref-Museum_of_Australian_Democracy_130-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Museum_of_Australian_Democracy-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> Opposition Leader Scullin forcefully attacked the government, blaming the Prime Minister for an industrial environment that was adversarial and punitive, stressing that Commonwealth arbitration had safeguarded many rights of workers and was completely workable with conciliation and proper consultation. Scullin also criticised the government for the growing debt problem and economic malaise, which for some years he had predicted would lead to a major economic crisis.<sup id="cite_ref-Scullin,_James_Henry_1876–1953_134-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Scullin,_James_Henry_1876–1953-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the event, the government was <a href="/wiki/1929_Australian_federal_election" title="1929 Australian federal election">soundly defeated on 12 October</a>, losing more than half its seats in parliament. To add to his humiliation, Bruce was defeated in his own seat of Flinders by Labor challenger <a href="/wiki/Jack_Holloway" title="Jack Holloway">Jack Holloway</a>. On paper, there was no indication that Bruce was in any danger of losing his seat; he held Flinders with a reasonably safe majority of 10.7 percent in 1928.  However, on the second count Holloway picked up enough preferences from an independent Liberal candidate to give him the victory.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> Bruce was the first sitting prime minister to lose his own seat, a feat which would not be repeated until 2007.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> He was measured in defeat however, stating, "The people have said they do not want my services, and I am going into the banishment to which they have sent me."<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Return_to_cabinet.2C_1931.E2.80.931933"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Return_to_cabinet,_1931–1933">Return to cabinet, 1931–1933</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Return to cabinet, 1931–1933">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Bruces_arriving_in_England,_1930s.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/The_Bruces_arriving_in_England%2C_1930s.jpg/220px-The_Bruces_arriving_in_England%2C_1930s.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="299" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/The_Bruces_arriving_in_England%2C_1930s.jpg/330px-The_Bruces_arriving_in_England%2C_1930s.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/The_Bruces_arriving_in_England%2C_1930s.jpg/440px-The_Bruces_arriving_in_England%2C_1930s.jpg 2x" data-file-width="441" data-file-height="600" /></a><figcaption>The Bruces arriving in England</figcaption></figure>
<p>Bruce returned to England after his defeat to holiday and attend to his business.  Sir John Latham, took over as leader of the Nationalists. With the <a href="/wiki/Stock_market_crash_of_1929" class="mw-redirect" title="Stock market crash of 1929">stock market crash in 1929</a> and the onset of the <a href="/wiki/Great_Depression_in_Australia" title="Great Depression in Australia">Great Depression in Australia</a>, Bruce commented to his colleagues that their defeat was probably fortunate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee95_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee95-151">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> But he ardently defended his government's record, maintaining that the economic crisis was inevitable and that his own policies were justified attempts to try to strengthen the Australian economy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee96_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee96-152">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> It was this defence that he mounted in April 1931 when he announced he would return to politics and seek to regain his seat of Flinders at the next election. By now the Nationalists had regrouped as the <a href="/wiki/United_Australia_Party" title="United Australia Party">United Australia Party</a> (UAP) under <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Lyons" title="Joseph Lyons">Joseph Lyons</a>, who had defected with several others from the embattled Scullin government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHendersonChapter_21_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHendersonChapter_21-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 1931 the Scullin government was defeated in parliament over its controversial <a href="/wiki/Premiers%27_Plan" title="Premiers&#39; Plan">Premiers' Plan</a>, catching Bruce by surprise as he was in England attending to Paterson, Laing and Bruce affairs when a <a href="/wiki/1931_Australian_federal_election" title="1931 Australian federal election">new election</a> was called. The Scullin government was subsequently defeated in a landslide, losing a then-record 32 seats; the two Labor factions were cut down to only 18 seats between them.  With Holloway having abandoned Flinders to run in the much friendlier <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Melbourne_Ports" title="Division of Melbourne Ports">Melbourne Ports</a>, Bruce was returned <i>in absentia</i> to his old seat, regaining it with a swing of 18.5 percent.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> He was appointed assistant treasurer in the new Lyons government, Lyons having taken the treasury portfolio personally.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee98_155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee98-155">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> Lyons leaned heavily on Bruce and Latham in his first six months of government, though Bruce had by now set his sights on international affairs rather than the domestic crisis.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee99_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee99-156">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bruce led the Australian delegation to the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire_Economic_Conference" title="British Empire Economic Conference">1932 Imperial Economic Conference</a> in Ottawa.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston98_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston98-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> He redoubled his efforts to improve Australian economic prospects with the empire, and the conference agreed to a limited form of his long-sought imperial preference scheme that would give Australia greater access and competitiveness in imperial markets over five years, an achievement for which Bruce received much praise from the Lyons government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston99–100_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston99–100-158">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> The closer trading arrangements of the <a href="/wiki/Ottawa_Agreements" class="mw-redirect" title="Ottawa Agreements">Ottawa Agreements</a> would set the pattern for Australian-British trade relations until Britain's entry in the <a href="/wiki/European_Common_Market" class="mw-redirect" title="European Common Market">European Common Market</a> in 1973.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards211–212_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards211–212-159">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the success of the Imperial Economic Conference, Lyons appointed Bruce to London as Resident Minister in the United Kingdom&#160;&#8211;&#32; it was to remain his and Ethel's home for the rest of their lives.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStirling148_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStirling148-160">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> His first task in London was to renegotiate the terms of Australia's burgeoning government debts, the repayment of which was crippling Depression-era Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards213–214_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards213–214-161">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> Over two years, he negotiated with the <a href="/wiki/Westminster_Bank" title="Westminster Bank">Westminster Bank</a> and the British Government for loan conversions worth £84&#160;million, which saved Australia millions of pounds in interest over several years and along with the Ottawa Agreements were significant in helping alleviate the Australian government's budgetary difficulties.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston103–106_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston103–106-162">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> Bruce was asked at several points during the 1930s to return to Australia by UAP backers and other political figures, who hoped that he could replace Lyons as prime minister. He himself had questioned Lyons' health and capacity to execute the role effectively, but he showed little interest in returning.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston106_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston106-163">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> In 1938 and 1939 Bruce was approached by senior UAP figures and Lyons himself to return to Australia and assume the leadership of the government, which was struggling under the ailing Lyons, but he either flatly declined or set conditions for his return (such as an all-party government behind him) that were impossible to meet.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHenderson426–427_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHenderson426–427-165">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="High_Commissioner_to_the_United_Kingdom.2C_1933.E2.80.931945"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="High_Commissioner_to_the_United_Kingdom,_1933–1945">High Commissioner to the United Kingdom, 1933–1945</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: High Commissioner to the United Kingdom, 1933–1945">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Stanley_Bruce_1934.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Stanley_Bruce_1934.jpg/220px-Stanley_Bruce_1934.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="279" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Stanley_Bruce_1934.jpg/330px-Stanley_Bruce_1934.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Stanley_Bruce_1934.jpg/440px-Stanley_Bruce_1934.jpg 2x" data-file-width="773" data-file-height="981" /></a><figcaption>Bruce in 1934</figcaption></figure>
<p>In September 1933, Bruce was appointed by Lyons to replace the ailing <a href="/wiki/Granville_Ryrie" title="Granville Ryrie">Sir Granville Ryrie</a> as <a href="/wiki/High_Commissioner_to_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="High Commissioner to the United Kingdom">High Commissioner to the United Kingdom</a>, giving him ambassadorial rank. He formally resigned from parliament on 7 October 1933.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> Bruce would excel in the new post, becoming a trusted confidant among Conservative politicians and a familiar face in British government circles, which led to him at one point considering entering British politics formally.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee120_167-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee120-167">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> Bruce was particularly close to Prime Minister Stanley Baldwin, with whom he frequently consulted, and was notably influential in pressing for <a href="/wiki/Edward_VIII_abdication_crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="Edward VIII abdication crisis">Edward VIII's abdication in 1936</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards249–254_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards249–254-168">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> His importance and power in London was due in part to the free rein that Lyons gave him&#160;&#8211;&#32; a rarity in the history of the position.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEP.G._Edwards39–41_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEP.G._Edwards39–41-169">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> Combined with the turmoil and frequent ministerial changes within the <a href="/wiki/Department_of_External_Affairs_(1921%E2%80%9370)" class="mw-redirect" title="Department of External Affairs (1921–70)">Commonwealth Department of External Affairs</a>, Bruce was generally credited as Australia's most influential and credible international representative during his posting, often determining matters of foreign policy in his own right.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEP.G._Edwards40–47_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEP.G._Edwards40–47-170">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="League_of_Nations">League of Nations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: League of Nations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bruce_presiding_over_the_League_of_Nations_Council.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Bruce_presiding_over_the_League_of_Nations_Council.png/220px-Bruce_presiding_over_the_League_of_Nations_Council.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="180" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Bruce_presiding_over_the_League_of_Nations_Council.png/330px-Bruce_presiding_over_the_League_of_Nations_Council.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Bruce_presiding_over_the_League_of_Nations_Council.png/440px-Bruce_presiding_over_the_League_of_Nations_Council.png 2x" data-file-width="887" data-file-height="727" /></a><figcaption>Bruce chairing the League of Nations Council in 1936. <a href="/wiki/Joachim_von_Ribbentrop" title="Joachim von Ribbentrop">Joachim von Ribbentrop</a> is addressing the council.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Bruce represented Australia at the League of Nations and successfully lobbied for Australia to participate as a member of the League Council from 1933 to 1936. He opposed action against <a href="/wiki/Empire_of_Japan" title="Empire of Japan">Japan</a> following the <a href="/wiki/Japanese_invasion_of_Manchuria" title="Japanese invasion of Manchuria">invasion of Manchuria</a> in 1933, concerned as to <a href="/wiki/Australia%E2%80%93Japan_relations" title="Australia–Japan relations">Australia's trading relationship with Japan</a> and the potential future threat it posed to peace in the Pacific.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHudson67–70_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHudson67–70-171">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup>  He also attempted to steer the League away from sanctioning member nations, believing it yet lacked the military or economic sway to do so effectively and feared the breakdown of the League&#160;&#8211;&#32; a prospect that loomed after <a href="/wiki/Nazi_Germany" title="Nazi Germany">Germany</a> and Japan departed the body in 1933.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHudson74–77_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHudson74–77-172">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> During the <a href="/wiki/Abyssinia_Crisis" title="Abyssinia Crisis">Abyssinia Crisis</a>, Bruce again counselled against partial sanctions, believing them the worst option as they would not stop the <a href="/wiki/Second_Italo-Ethiopian_War" title="Second Italo-Ethiopian War">Ethiopian invasion</a> and yet would alienate Italy&#160;&#8211;&#32; then a <a href="/wiki/Stresa_Front" title="Stresa Front">potential ally</a> against a rearming Nazi Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHudson74–77_172-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHudson74–77-172">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> He further argued for much greater rearmament efforts in the United Kingdom and France to provide greater military capacity to enforce future decisions by the League. Bruce assumed the presidency of the League of Nations Council in 1936 at the height of the crisis and after the failure of the <a href="/wiki/Hoare%E2%80%93Laval_Pact" title="Hoare–Laval Pact">Hoare–Laval Pact</a> between France, Italy and Britain, but further attempts to forestall the invasion failed. He presided as League Council President during the <a href="/wiki/Rhineland_Crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="Rhineland Crisis">Rhineland Crisis</a>, although once again attempts to respond to fascist aggression failed. Although this did not shake his conviction in the potential of the League, he saw it doomed to failure without fundamental reforms to its structure and system of sanctions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston128–129_173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston128–129-173">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> He was nominated by Turkey to chair the 1936 <a href="/wiki/Montreux_Convention_Regarding_the_Regime_of_the_Straits" title="Montreux Convention Regarding the Regime of the Straits">Montreux Conference</a>, which was far more successful in negotiating international agreement on passage through the <a href="/wiki/Turkish_Straits" class="mw-redirect" title="Turkish Straits">Turkish Straits</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32; an issue of particular relevance to Bruce as a veteran of the Gallipoli campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston129–130_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston129–130-174">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> Despite the turmoil of his presidency, the League historian F.P. Walters would later describe Bruce as "the best, perhaps, of the many first-rate chairmen who presided over the Council, Conferences, or Committees of the League".<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
By 1937 Bruce's attention had shifted to social and economic co-operation, which he believed had far greater potential for success and was of greater importance to humanity at large.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston129–130_174-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston129–130-174">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> He had taken a leading role in promoting agriculture, nutrition and economic co-operation through the League of Nations, working intensively with <a href="/wiki/Frank_L._McDougall" class="mw-redirect" title="Frank L. McDougall">Frank L. McDougall</a> and <a href="/wiki/John_Boyd_Orr" title="John Boyd Orr">John Boyd Orr</a> throughout the decade.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee112–113_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee112–113-176">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> In 1937 he presented a plan of "economic appeasement", which built on this work and aimed to ease international tensions by reviving international trade and improving living standards in Europe through better working conditions, lower food prices, rural credits and housing assistance. Barriers to trade would gradually be reduced while European nations still recovering from the depression would be reintegrated into the international economy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee122–123_177-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee122–123-177">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> In doing so he made a firm link between international trade and international peace, believing it key to unlocking world economic potential. Foreshadowing the logic of the <a href="/wiki/Marshall_Plan" title="Marshall Plan">Marshall Plan</a>, Bruce argued that unrelieved economic and social hardship threatened to push other nations towards fascism or communism.<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> </p><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bruce_chairing_the_Montreaux_Conference.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Bruce_chairing_the_Montreaux_Conference.jpg/220px-Bruce_chairing_the_Montreaux_Conference.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="173" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Bruce_chairing_the_Montreaux_Conference.jpg/330px-Bruce_chairing_the_Montreaux_Conference.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/3/3a/Bruce_chairing_the_Montreaux_Conference.jpg 2x" data-file-width="425" data-file-height="335" /></a><figcaption>Bruce as Chairman of the Montreux Conference, 1938</figcaption></figure>
<blockquote><p>I feel very strongly that it will be impossible to find a solution to the political problems of Europe and remove the present nightmare conditions unless something is done to improve the economic position&#160;... it is vital for the prestige and future wellbeing of the League that it should afford active leadership towards bringing about economic appeasement.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston150_179-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston150-179">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>The plan was supported by Secretary-General <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Louis_Anne_Avenol" class="mw-redirect" title="Joseph Louis Anne Avenol">Joseph Louis Anne Avenol</a>, who like Bruce recognised that the League was rapidly becoming moribund and that a major change of direction was needed, although neither was successful in convincing key states in contributing to the plan.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStirling131_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStirling131-180">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHudson172–173_181-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHudson172–173-181">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> Critically, new British Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Neville_Chamberlain" title="Neville Chamberlain">Neville Chamberlain</a> could not be convinced by Bruce to invest further into the development of the League, and the body began to lose its political impetus as war loomed. He would continue to press for League reform in the lead up to the war. The Bruce committee to advise on League reform was formed in 1939 in the aftermath of the <a href="/wiki/German_occupation_of_Czechoslovakia" class="mw-redirect" title="German occupation of Czechoslovakia">partition of Czechoslovakia</a> by Nazi Germany and the apparent failure of Chamberlain's <a href="/wiki/Appeasement" title="Appeasement">appeasement</a> policy. This committee, which met in July and August 1939, proposed a significant expansion of Bruce's earlier ideas to the League, bringing a wide range of economic and social programs under its purview as a means of fostering international co-operation. Their work, however, would be rendered moot by the outbreak of World War II.<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="World_War_II">World War II</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: World War II">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In the events leading up to World War II, Bruce and Lyons had been supporters of the British under Chamberlain and the policy of appeasement exercised with regards to the <a href="/wiki/Reoccupation_of_the_Rhineland" class="mw-redirect" title="Reoccupation of the Rhineland">reoccupation of the Rhineland</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Anschluss" title="Anschluss">Anschluss</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Munich_Crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="Munich Crisis">Munich Crisis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> Even during the "<a href="/wiki/Phoney_war" class="mw-redirect" title="Phoney war">phoney war</a>", Bruce continued to advocate the implementation of a more durable international system to enforce peace through mutual disarmament, the expansion of transnational trade, and global organisations capable of addressing the pressing social and economic questions that he felt were the recurrent causes of international conflict.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee138_184-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee138-184">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> He had become a close confidant of senior Conservative Party figures <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Eden" title="Anthony Eden">Anthony Eden</a> and Neville Chamberlain in this period,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStirling60_185-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStirling60-185">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> and was strident in advancing the opinion of Australia (and the dominions more broadly) that negotiation and compromise with Nazi Germany was preferable to war.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston157–167_186-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston157–167-186">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> Bruce actively participated in the negotiations for the <a href="/wiki/Munich_Agreement" title="Munich Agreement">Munich Agreement</a>. When Lyons died in April 1939, Earle Page and Richard Casey personally appealed for Bruce to return to Australia and take over once more as prime minister at the head of the UAP. Bruce demurred, however, and made it the condition of his return that he be allowed to sit in parliament as an <a href="/wiki/Independent_(politician)" class="mw-redirect" title="Independent (politician)">independent</a> and lead an all-party <a href="/wiki/Unity_government" class="mw-redirect" title="Unity government">unity government</a>. Such conditions were politically impossible to meet, and Robert Menzies <a href="/wiki/1939_United_Australia_Party_leadership_election" title="1939 United Australia Party leadership election">was elected as the new leader of the UAP</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston168–169_187-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston168–169-187">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Stanley_Bruce_and_the_Troops.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Stanley_Bruce_and_the_Troops.png/310px-Stanley_Bruce_and_the_Troops.png" decoding="async" width="310" height="223" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Stanley_Bruce_and_the_Troops.png/465px-Stanley_Bruce_and_the_Troops.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Stanley_Bruce_and_the_Troops.png/620px-Stanley_Bruce_and_the_Troops.png 2x" data-file-width="1328" data-file-height="954" /></a><figcaption>High Commissioner Bruce drinking tea with Australian troops in London during World War II</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Declaration_of_war_by_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Declaration of war by the United Kingdom">Britain's declaration of war</a> against Germany on 3 September 1939 was followed within hours by Australia's Prime Minister Menzies stating that because of Britain's declaration Australia too was now at war with Germany. Winston Churchill's accession as British Prime Minister in May 1940 brought Bruce into frequent conflict with the British government. Churchill saw the dominions as still semi-dependent colonies who were at London's command<sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> whereas Bruce saw the Empire as a kind of international partnership and the dominions as rightful parties to the decision-making process.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee137_189-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee137-189">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> Britain's preoccupation with the European theatre alarmed Australian politicians, given the tenuous position of Far Eastern possessions and the looming possibility of Japanese invasion.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston180–184_190-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston180–184-190">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After <a href="/wiki/Pacific_War" title="Pacific War">a string of defeats in the Far East</a>, particularly the <a href="/wiki/Fall_of_Singapore" title="Fall of Singapore">Fall of Singapore</a>, the Australian government was finally successful in having Bruce accredited to the <a href="/wiki/British_War_Cabinet" class="mw-redirect" title="British War Cabinet">British War Cabinet</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pacific_War_Council" title="Pacific War Council">Pacific War Council</a> as an Australian (and dominion) representative.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston196–198_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston196–198-191">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> 
However, Bruce soon became embroiled in the disputes over Churchill's autocratic leadership style and his lack of consultation with the cabinet over war decisions. He was regularly left out of cabinet communique or not invited to meetings, much to his displeasure.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEP.G._Edwards52_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEP.G._Edwards52-192">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> With the fear of <a href="/wiki/Proposed_Japanese_invasion_of_Australia_during_World_War_II" title="Proposed Japanese invasion of Australia during World War II">Japanese invasion</a> mounting in Australia throughout 1942, Bruce directly confronted Churchill on a number of occasions over Far East policy and the continuing lack of consultation with Australia and his own cabinet. Churchill usually responded by rebuffing him or pointedly excluding him further from government business.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards275–285_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards275–285-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> Although outwardly relenting in the face of pressure exerted by the dominions for representation in war decisions, Churchill routinely marginalised or ignored that representation.<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> Bruce persisted in this difficult arrangement until May 1944 when he became completely disillusioned and resigned, choosing other forums in which to represent Australia in London.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston220_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston220-195">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> In spite of his tempestuous relationship with Churchill, Bruce was held in high regard by many cabinet members, particularly future prime ministers <a href="/wiki/Clement_Attlee" title="Clement Attlee">Clement Attlee</a> and Anthony Eden, and his dogged determination to advance dominion interests during the war years earned him high praise from <a href="/wiki/John_Curtin" title="John Curtin">John Curtin</a> and the other dominion prime ministers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee170–176_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee170–176-196">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Food_and_Agriculture_Organization.2C_1946.E2.80.931951"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Food_and_Agriculture_Organization,_1946–1951">Food and Agriculture Organization, 1946–1951</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Food and Agriculture Organization, 1946–1951">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Pius_XII_and_the_World_Food_Council_1950.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Pius_XII_and_the_World_Food_Council_1950.jpg/220px-Pius_XII_and_the_World_Food_Council_1950.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="166" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Pius_XII_and_the_World_Food_Council_1950.jpg/330px-Pius_XII_and_the_World_Food_Council_1950.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Pius_XII_and_the_World_Food_Council_1950.jpg/440px-Pius_XII_and_the_World_Food_Council_1950.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3314" data-file-height="2505" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a> with chairman Bruce and members of the World Food Council in Rome, 1950</figcaption></figure>
<p>By the war's end in 1945, Bruce had become tired of the High Commission posting and hinted to Curtin's successor <a href="/wiki/Ben_Chifley" title="Ben Chifley">Ben Chifley</a> that he would not object to being replaced in the position.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee162_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee162-197">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> In the last years of the war he had envisaged a post-war order based on a continuing alliance of the four powers&#160;&#8211;&#32; the United States, the British Empire, the Soviet Union and <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_China_(1912%E2%80%9349)" class="mw-redirect" title="Republic of China (1912–49)">China</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32; that could evolve into a new international body with a similar function as the League of Nations, but with a stronger role and authority in international matters.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee166–168_198-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee166–168-198">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> Bruce had rejoined Frank McDougall and John Boyd Orr in these years in reviving their proposals for international co-operation on nutrition and agriculture. He wrote and made representations at all levels on the subject, and became a leading voice once more advocating the creation of an international body to examine social and economic questions, much as he had done during his years with the League of Nations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStirling280–283_199-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStirling280–283-199">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> The efforts of McDougall, Bruce and Orr finally paid off when their work came to the attention of <a href="/wiki/Eleanor_Roosevelt" title="Eleanor Roosevelt">Eleanor Roosevelt</a>, and subsequently the American government, and their proposals would find expression at the Food and Agriculture Conference that <a href="/wiki/Franklin_Roosevelt" class="mw-redirect" title="Franklin Roosevelt">Franklin Roosevelt</a> convened in <a href="/wiki/Hot_Springs,_Virginia" title="Hot Springs, Virginia">Hot Springs, Virginia</a>, in May 1943. This conference agreed to the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/Food_and_Agriculture_Organization" title="Food and Agriculture Organization">Food and Agriculture Organization</a> (FAO), which became an associated institution with the United Nations when it was established formally in October 1945.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee170–171_200-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee170–171-200">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As the war in Europe drew to a close and the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Charter" class="mw-redirect" title="United Nations Charter">United Nations Charter</a> was promulgated in June 1945, Bruce's name was among those being considered to become the first <a href="/wiki/Secretary-General_of_the_United_Nations" title="Secretary-General of the United Nations">Secretary-General of the United Nations</a>, with American Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/Dean_Acheson" title="Dean Acheson">Dean Acheson</a> and <a href="/wiki/British_Foreign_Secretary" class="mw-redirect" title="British Foreign Secretary">British Foreign Secretary</a> Anthony Eden actively supporting his candidacy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee171–172_201-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee171–172-201">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> Bruce, however, was now 62 and indicated that he felt himself too old for the position, instead preferring a less demanding role considering economic and social questions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHasluck246_202-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasluck246-202">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> In 1946 he assumed the chairmanship of the FAO Preparatory Commission on World Food Proposals, which had the mission of establishing a "world food board" to coordinate international policy on nutrition and develop a system to eliminate global agricultural shortages.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston250_203-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston250-203">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> He proposed many international schemes as part of this work, particularly a <a href="/wiki/Food_bank" title="Food bank">world food reserve</a> and special pricing mechanisms to reallocate and release food to where it was most needed in times of need.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston250_203-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston250-203">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> Bruce's Commission also placed a high emphasis on agricultural modernisation, <a href="/wiki/International_aid" class="mw-redirect" title="International aid">international development aid</a>, commodity agreements and price stability to help starving developing nations in its findings presented in 1947.<sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> The proposals were never adopted, as the costs and challenges to national sovereignty proved too difficult to overcome politically.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee176–177_205-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee176–177-205">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Undeterred, Bruce was elected Chairman of the newly formed FAO Council in November 1947, working once more with John Boyd Orr, now Secretary-General of the FAO. Acute shortages of cereals and livestock were rampant following severe droughts in Europe, and the international food supply system was under serious strain after the devastation of the war. Bruce and the council worked in these years to distribute <a href="/wiki/Fertiliser" class="mw-redirect" title="Fertiliser">fertiliser</a> and <a href="/wiki/Agricultural_machinery" title="Agricultural machinery">agricultural machinery</a>, as well as improve nutrition, especially in less developed nations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston252_206-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston252-206">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup> More than two-thirds of the world was undernourished in 1949<sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup> and Bruce felt it imperative for the council to bring these stark facts to the developed nations.<sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> A landmark agreement on technical aid between the FAO and the United Nations was reached in November 1949, and the FAO received the funding and logistical capacity to act on the food shortage crisis and the problems of poor nutrition in the developing world. Bruce and the FAO were successful in these years in supporting the recovery of world agricultural output. By 1951 this had exceeded pre-war levels, and general levels of nutrition were rising internationally, but by the time Bruce stepped down in that year neither had improved fast enough to keep pace with the <a href="/wiki/Post-World_War_II_baby_boom" class="mw-redirect" title="Post-World War II baby boom">post-war population boom</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> The Council faced major obstacles in supporting the improvement of conditions in the developing world as governments there began diverting resources to arms programs as independence, post-colonial and <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a> conflicts multiplied.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston253_210-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston253-210">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> Frustrated by continuing world conflict and the lack of commitment from the developed world to support the lofty but very difficult aims of the FAO, both Bruce and Orr resigned from the FAO disappointed by its modest gains and insufficient powers to alleviate world food problems.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards422–423_211-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards422–423-211">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_life">Later life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Later life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Coat_of_Arms_of_Stanley_Melbourne_Bruce,_1st_Viscount_Bruce_of_Melbourne.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fc/Coat_of_Arms_of_Stanley_Melbourne_Bruce%2C_1st_Viscount_Bruce_of_Melbourne.svg/220px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Stanley_Melbourne_Bruce%2C_1st_Viscount_Bruce_of_Melbourne.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="311" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fc/Coat_of_Arms_of_Stanley_Melbourne_Bruce%2C_1st_Viscount_Bruce_of_Melbourne.svg/330px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Stanley_Melbourne_Bruce%2C_1st_Viscount_Bruce_of_Melbourne.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fc/Coat_of_Arms_of_Stanley_Melbourne_Bruce%2C_1st_Viscount_Bruce_of_Melbourne.svg/440px-Coat_of_Arms_of_Stanley_Melbourne_Bruce%2C_1st_Viscount_Bruce_of_Melbourne.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="744" data-file-height="1052" /></a><figcaption>Arms of Viscount Bruce of Melbourne <i>Or, a saltire gules cantoned between four mullets sable, on a chief of the second a pale argent charged with a saltire of the third</i><sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Bruce occupied a range of positions in his later years, sharing his time between the United Kingdom and Australia. He had been Chairman of the Finance Corporation of Industry since 1946 and continued in the role until 1957, providing finance to projects of benefit to the British national economy. Bruce helped establish the program in Australia in 1954 and on a Commonwealth basis in 1956.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston253–256_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston253–256-213">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> He became the first <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_(education)" title="Chancellor (education)">Chancellor</a> of the newly established <a href="/wiki/Australian_National_University" title="Australian National University">Australian National University</a> in 1952, and took an active interest in its development, especially as a research centre for the study of Asia and the Pacific.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee180–184_214-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee180–184-214">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> Bruce concluded that Australia's position in the world had changed as a result of World War II, commenting:
</p>
<blockquote><p>[Australia] has become a bridgehead between East and West. It is now vital that Australia should understand the problems of the East, that she should do whatever is in her power to alleviate those problems, and that she should interpret the nature of those problems to the rest of the world.<sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>The residential college Bruce Hall was named in his honour, and he remained active in the life of the university until his retirement from the position in 1961.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee179–182_216-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee179–182-216">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup> Bruce sat as director on many corporate boards in retirement, notably the <a href="/wiki/National_Bank_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="National Bank of Australia">National Bank of Australia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Peninsular_and_Oriental_Steam_Navigation_Company" class="mw-redirect" title="Peninsular and Oriental Steam Navigation Company">P&amp;O</a> and the National Mutual Life Association.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston253–256_213-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston253–256-213">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> In 1947 he became the first Australian to sit in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a> (<a href="/wiki/John_Forrest" title="John Forrest">Sir John Forrest</a> had been granted a peerage but died before it could be invested).<sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Having been elevated to the peerage as <i>Viscount Bruce of Melbourne</i> by long-time colleague Clement Attlee, he would be an active participant in the chamber, attending regularly until his death. Bruce used it as a platform to continue to campaign on international and national social and economic questions, and to promote recognition and representation for Australia within the Commonwealth, though by this time Australian and British interests were becoming increasingly far apart, and the British Empire was rapidly disintegrating.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston259–262_218-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston259–262-218">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup> He also continued to lobby the British government in these years to increase its commitment to third world development and the FAO. An avid golfer his whole life, Bruce became the first Australian captain of the <a href="/wiki/The_Royal_and_Ancient_Golf_Club_of_St_Andrews" title="The Royal and Ancient Golf Club of St Andrews">Royal and Ancient Golf Club of St Andrews</a> in 1954. From 1948 to 1952 he was President of <a href="/wiki/Leander_Club" title="Leander Club">Leander Club</a>, while continuing to coach rowing at Cambridge University sporadically, and frequently appeared at public events both in Australia and in England.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards448–452_219-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards448–452-219">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Stanley_Bruce_bust.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Stanley_Bruce_bust.jpg/220px-Stanley_Bruce_bust.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Stanley_Bruce_bust.jpg/330px-Stanley_Bruce_bust.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Stanley_Bruce_bust.jpg/440px-Stanley_Bruce_bust.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="1600" /></a><figcaption>Bust of Stanley Bruce by sculptor Wallace Anderson located in the <a href="/wiki/Prime_Ministers_Avenue" title="Prime Ministers Avenue">Prime Ministers Avenue</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Ballarat_Botanical_Gardens" title="Ballarat Botanical Gardens">Ballarat Botanical Gardens</a> in <a href="/wiki/Ballarat" title="Ballarat">Ballarat</a>, <a href="/wiki/Victoria_(Australia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Victoria (Australia)">Victoria</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Bruce remained active and in good health right through his retirement despite the gradual onset of <a href="/wiki/Deafness" title="Deafness">deafness</a>, but the death of his wife Ethel in March 1967 took a deep toll on him. He died on 25 August 1967 at the age of 84. He was the last surviving member of <a href="/wiki/Billy_Hughes" title="Billy Hughes">Billy Hughes</a>' Cabinet. His memorial service was held at <a href="/wiki/St_Martin-in-the-Fields" title="St Martin-in-the-Fields">St Martin-in-the-Fields</a> and was widely attended, including by representatives of the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Windsor" title="House of Windsor">Royal Family</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECumpston265_220-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECumpston265-220">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup> His ashes were scattered over <a href="/wiki/Lake_Burley_Griffin" title="Lake Burley Griffin">Lake Burley Griffin</a> in Canberra.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee183_221-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee183-221">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup> The Canberra suburb of <a href="/wiki/Bruce,_Australian_Capital_Territory" title="Bruce, Australian Capital Territory">Bruce</a>, and the electoral <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Bruce" title="Division of Bruce">Division of Bruce</a> based in south-east Melbourne, were both named for him.<sup id="cite_ref-222" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-222">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-223" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-223">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy_and_evaluation">Legacy and evaluation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Legacy and evaluation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_Prime_Ministers_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Historical rankings of Prime Ministers of Australia">Historical rankings of Prime Ministers of Australia</a></div>
<p>Despite his many accomplishments both at home and abroad, Bruce's post-prime ministerial career was not well known in Australia, and most still harboured memories of his harsh anti-union legislation and his government's landslide defeat in 1929.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee173_224-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee173-224">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup> His public persona was one of an aloof man, too English for Australia in style and bearing.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography_20-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Dictionary_of_Biography-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Upon his death in 1967, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Age" title="The Age">The Age</a></i> of his hometown Melbourne remarked that "for most Australians, he is little more than a shadow."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStirling459–460_225-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStirling459–460-225">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> Bruce spent much of his life and career in the United Kingdom, the country that conversely held him in high regard, but never forgot his Australian roots and for much of his career was a tireless advocate for its interests. In contrast to his image as a member of the British aristocratic elite, he spent much of his later career working for solutions to the problems facing the world's poorest.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStirling458–461_226-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStirling458–461-226">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bruce was high-minded and ambitious in setting an agenda&#160;&#8211;&#32; as prime minister he pursued complex and aspiring schemes of economic, social and administrative development, including grandiose solutions to the problem of industrial relations and an egalitarian reworking of the British Empire. In his diplomatic career he pursued better treatment for the Commonwealth and programs through the League of Nations and United Nations that would address world questions of pressing social and economic concern, culminating in his most ambitious work to eliminate world hunger through the Food and Agriculture Organization. The Australian government even nominated Bruce for the <a href="/wiki/Nobel_Peace_Prize" title="Nobel Peace Prize">Nobel Peace Prize</a> in recognition for these efforts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHenderson352_227-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHenderson352-227">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup> Yet his schemes frequently bordered on the idealistic, and he was frequently disappointed at the limited degree to which he could practically implement his ideas. As Bruce himself would concede in later life, he was overly ambitious by nature and "forever buying into things that aren't really my concern".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStirling488_228-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStirling488-228">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> But despite his lack of public recognition in Australia, peers and historians have long recognised the abiding impact Bruce had both as prime minister and internationalist, leading his successor as Chancellor of the Australian National University Sir <a href="/wiki/John_Cockcroft" title="John Cockcroft">John Cockcroft</a> to conclude in 1962 that Bruce was "probably the outstanding Australian of our time".<sup id="cite_ref-229" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-229">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> The <i>Melbourne Sun</i> agreed with the assessment, stating upon his death that Bruce was "probably the least remembered but the most extraordinary of our Prime Ministers".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStirling459–460_225-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStirling459–460-225">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Biographies">Biographies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Bruce&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Biographies">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
